Parameter Estimation with Increased Precision for Elliptic and Hypo-elliptic Diffusions
Yuga Iguchi, Alexandros Beskos, Matthew Graham

Abstract
This work aims at making a comprehensive contribution in the general area of parametric inference for discretely observed diffusion processes. Established approaches for likelihood-based estimation invoke a time-discretisation scheme for the approximation of the intractable transition dynamics of the Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE) model over finite time periods. The scheme is applied for a step-size δ > 0 \displaystyle\delta>0 , that is either user-selected or determined by the data. Recent research has highlighted the critical effect of the choice of numerical scheme on the behaviour of derived parameter estimates in the setting of hypo-elliptic SDEs. In brief, in our work, first, we develop two weak second order sampling schemes (to cover both hypo-elliptic and elliptic SDEs) and produce a small time expansion for the density of the schemes to form a proxy for the true intractable SDE transition density. Then, we establish a collection of analytic results for likelihood-based parameter estimates obtained via the formed proxies, thus providing a theoretical framework that showcases advantages from the use of the developed methodology for SDE calibration. We present numerical results from carrying out classical or Bayesian inference, for both elliptic and hypo-elliptic SDEs.



Keywords:
CLT; data augmentation; hypo-elliptic diffusion; small time density expansion; stochastic differential equation.

1
Introduction

Our work is placed within the general framework of parametric inference for diffusion processes.
Calibration approaches under broad observation regimes, in both Bayesian and classical settings, necessitate the use of a numerical scheme used as proxy for the underlying, typically intractable, Markovian dynamics of the model over finite time steps. Following the latest contributions in the area, we aim to make connections between approximation schemes (i.e., a research area mainly within the remit of stochastic analysis) and their impact on the accuracy and performance of induced likelihood-based inferential approaches (i.e., the field of statistical calibration for Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs)).

Let
\displaystyle(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space and
\displaystyle B=(B_{1,t},\ldots,B_{d_{R},t})_{t\geq 0}
a standard
\displaystyle d_{R}
-dimensional Brownian motion defined thereon,
\displaystyle d_{R}\geq 1
. We use the convention
\displaystyle B_{0,t}=t
. Consider the following general class of SDEs:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dX_{t}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}dX_{R,t}\\
dX_{S,t}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}V_{R,0}(X_{t},\beta)\\
V_{S,0}(X_{t},\gamma)\end{array}\right]dt+\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}V_{R,k}(X_{t},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{0}_{d_{S}}\end{array}\right]\,dB_{k,t},\qquad X_{0}=x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{aligned}

(1.1)

with
\displaystyle V_{R,0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{\beta}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}}
,
\displaystyle V_{S,0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{\gamma}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{S}}
,
\displaystyle V_{R,k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{\sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}}
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq N
, where
\displaystyle d_{S}\geq 0
and
\displaystyle d=d_{R}+d_{S}
.
Also,
\displaystyle\Theta_{\beta}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\beta}}
,
\displaystyle\Theta_{\gamma}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}}
,
\displaystyle\Theta_{\sigma}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\sigma}}
,
\displaystyle d_{\beta}\geq 1
,
\displaystyle d_{\gamma}\geq 0
,
\displaystyle d_{\sigma}\geq 1
. We set
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)
and
\displaystyle\Theta=\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\gamma}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
.
\displaystyle X_{R,t}
and
\displaystyle X_{S,t}
denote the rough and smooth components, respectively, of process
\displaystyle X_{t}
that solves (
1.1
). For
\displaystyle d_{S}>0
,
\displaystyle\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a
hypo-elliptic diffusion
if the law of
\displaystyle X_{t}
,
\displaystyle t>0
, admits a density with respect to (w.r.t.) the Lebesgue measure, while for
\displaystyle d_{S}=0
the process is an elliptic one. For such a diffusion model,
we consider the problem of estimating
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
given observations for state
\displaystyle X_{t}
at the discrete-time instances

\displaystyle 0\leq t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n-1}<t_{n}
,
\displaystyle n\geq 0
. For simplicity, we assume that observation times are equidistant and set

\displaystyle\Delta:=t_{i}-t_{i-1}
,
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n
.

Non-linear SDEs do not permit, in general, analytical solutions, thus one must rely on approximate, time-discretisation schemes to generate diffusion sample paths and obtain closed-form expressions for the SDE transition density. Development of approximation schemes with high accuracy can lead to effective parameter estimation methods, e.g. to Bayesian data augmentation without excessive imputation of latent variables to cover a non-small step-size
\displaystyle\Delta
. Indeed, several works on Markov chain Monte-Carlo (MCMC) methods for diffusion processes suggest use of high order approximations, e.g. the Milstein or a strong 1.5 order scheme
(
Kloeden and Platen 1992
)
, but practical application of such schemes is restricted to limited classes of SDEs due to involvement of intractable random variables, such as the
Lévy area

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{0}^{\Delta}(B_{k_{1},s}dB_{k_{2},s}-B_{k_{2},s}dB_{k_{1},s}}),\,k_{1}\neq k_{2}
. Motivated by the above, we develop our work under the following strategy. First, we propose an explicit sampling scheme for the generation of sample paths for model (
1.1
), with higher order accuracy in distributional sense compared to classical (conditionally) Gaussian numerical schemes, e.g. the Euler-Maruyama scheme. Then, we derive an Edgeworth-type density expansion of the above sampling scheme. Instances of the expansion will be used to provide proxies for the true intractable transition density and, ultimately, likelihood-based parameter estimates. Finally, we provide analytic results showcasing advantages of the obtained parameter estimates in both high/low-frequency observation regimes, where in the former case one assumes
\displaystyle\Delta\rightarrow 0
and in the latter that
\displaystyle\Delta
is fixed and not small enough.

One set of analytic results that we provide in this work – and in accordance with recent contributions involving hypo-elliptic SDEs by, e.g.,
Ditlevsen and Samson 2019
;
Gloter and Yoshida 2021
– will correspond to asymptotic results for the Maximum Likelihood Estimator (MLE), in the high-frequency regime, with
\displaystyle n\rightarrow\infty
and
\displaystyle\Delta=\Delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
, and in a setting of
complete
discrete-time observations. That is, the dataset
\displaystyle Y^{c}
is
as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle Y^{c}:=\{X_{t_{0}},X_{t_{1}},\ldots,X_{t_{n}}\}.

(1.2)

We refer to the dataset in (
1.2
) in the high-frequency setting as the ‘complete observation regime’ in the sequel.
We stress that the discretisation schemes developed in the above context are practically relevant for generic observation regimes, e.g. within Bayesian data augmentation methods. Consideration of these latter methods motivates the derivation of further analytic results, now in a low-frequency regime. In particular, for elliptic SDEs,
we explicitly connect the weak order of the numerical scheme with the proximity between the true transition density over a period of size
\displaystyle\Delta
and the transition density produced by convolution of
\displaystyle M\geq 1
proxy transition densities over time intervals of size
\displaystyle\delta=\Delta/M
.

We briefly review recent works in the high-frequency regime. In the hypo-elliptic setting, a first main contribution is the work of
Pokern et al. 2009
that uses an Itô-Taylor expansion to add a noise term of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta^{3/2})
in the numerical scheme for the smooth component of SDE (
1.1
), thus obtaining a non-degenerate (conditionally) Gaussian approximation of the true transition density. The class of models considered in
Pokern et al. 2009
is restrictive and no analytical results are provided.

Ditlevsen and Samson 2019
make a major contribution by starting with a strong 1.5 order scheme before removing terms that do not affect their asymptotic results.
Noise of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta^{3/2})
is propagated onto the smooth component (as in
Pokern et al. 2009
) and quantities of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta^{2})
are retained in the mean terms. These latter components remove the bias for the estimates of drift parameters observed experimentally (within a Bayesian data augmentation setting) in
Pokern et al. 2009
.
Ditlevsen and Samson 2019
provide analytic asymptotic results for a contrast estimator in the complete observation regime (
1.2
), under the condition
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
; this is a type of condition referred to as ‘rapidly increasing experimental design’ in early investigations for elliptic SDEs in
Prakasa Rao 1988
. The estimation procedure in
Ditlevsen and Samson 2019
is separated into two contrast functions, one for

\displaystyle\gamma
assuming knowledge of the true values of
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)
, and vice-versa for the contrast function for
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)
. The analysis provides marginal CLTs for the estimates of the parameters rather than an ideal joint CLT. The class of models covered is restricted to a scalar smooth component and diagonal diffusion coefficient matrix for the rough component.

Gloter and Yoshida 2020
;
Gloter and Yoshida 2021
provide the most recent contributions. Closer to our purposes,
Gloter and Yoshida 2020
describe a non-adaptive approach (as opposed to one-step adaptive methods in
Gloter and Yoshida 2021
) and
prove a CLT in the complete observation regime for
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
, as in
Ditlevsen and Samson 2019
, but without strong restrictions on the class of models.
In the elliptic setting,
Kessler 1997
developed contrast functions for the scalar case that, within regime (
1.2
), deliver estimates satisfying a CLT for
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/q})
, for any integer
\displaystyle q\geq 2
.
The method in
Kessler 1997
is based on the use of a Gaussian density for the approximation of the transition density of the SDE for small
\displaystyle\Delta
, together with high order expansions in
\displaystyle\Delta
for the mean and variance of the SDE transitions.

Uchida and Yoshida 2012
extended such results to general model dimension based on multi-step adaptive estimates.

Our main contributions can be summarised as follows:

(a)

In the hypo-elliptic case
we propose a new weak second order sampling scheme. The scheme is explicit
as one can produce SDE sample paths by generating Gaussian variates.
A related weak second order scheme is also put forward for the elliptic case.

(b)

The above sampling scheme possess, in general, an intractable transition density
due to involvement of polynomials of Gaussian variates.
We derive a closed-form
small time density expansion
of the scheme by making use of tools from Malliavin calculus.

(c)

In the high-frequency regime, we develop our contrast function by selecting appropriate high order terms from the density expansion.
We then prove a joint CLT for the deduced parameter estimates under
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
, which is the largest step-size permitted in the hypo-elliptic case to the best of our knowledge. In particular, a main improvement by the new estimator – when compared with the existing estimator satisfying the CLT under
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
– is observed in the estimation of diffusion parameter
\displaystyle\sigma
.

(d)

In the low-frequency regime,
we choose particular
high order terms from the small time density expansion to develop our local weak third order transition density scheme.
We then study the use of the developed scheme in the practical setting
where its density is iteratively
applied with user-specified steps of size
\displaystyle\delta=\Delta/M
,
\displaystyle M\geq 1
, to cover fixed inter-observation times of length
\displaystyle\Delta
. We prove that the induced bias is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M^{-2})
for elliptic SDEs.

(e)

We show numerical examples illustrating the benefits of the new schemes in applications with hypo-elliptic SDEs in the high-frequency regime. In the context of Bayesian data augmentation, in the low-frequency regime,
we apply the sampling scheme to an elliptic model and observe that the bias
in the induced posterior is reduced when comparing with the Euler-Maruyama scheme.

The remaining part of the paper is organised as follows.
Section
2
puts forward our sampling schemes for elliptic and hypo-elliptic SDEs.
Section
3
derives the closed-form small time density expansion for the sampling scheme. Section
4
provides a collection of analytical results for parameter inference carried out via appropriate choice of high order terms from the developed density expansion,
both in the high and low-frequency regimes.
Section
5
provides numerical experiments related to the analytic results.
Section
6
concludes our work.

Notation.

We define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{0}(\cdot,\beta,\gamma)=\big[\,V_{R,0}(\cdot,\beta)^{\top},V_{S,0}(\cdot,\gamma)^{\top}\,\big]^{\top},\quad V_{k}(\cdot,\sigma)=\big[\,V_{R,k}(\cdot,\sigma)^{\top},\mathbf{0}_{d_{S}}^{\top}\,\big]^{\top},\quad 1\leq k\leq d_{R}.

We set

\displaystyle V_{R}=V_{R}(x,\sigma)=\big[V_{R,1}(x,\sigma),\ldots,V_{R,d_{R}}(x,\sigma)\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}\times d_{R}}
, and define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle a_{R}(x,\sigma)=V_{R}(x,\sigma)V_{R}(x,\sigma)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}\times d_{R}}.

(1.3)

Let

\displaystyle C_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})

(resp.
\displaystyle C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
),
\displaystyle m,n\geq 1
, be the space of smooth (i.e. infinitely differentiable) bounded (resp. of polynomial growth) functions
\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
with bounded derivatives (resp. with derivatives of polynomial growth).
We write
\displaystyle\textstyle\partial_{u}=\big[\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u^{1}},\ldots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u^{n}}\big]^{\top},\;\partial^{2}_{u}=\partial_{u}\partial_{u}^{\top}\equiv\big(\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{i}\partial u^{j}}\big)_{i,j=1}^{n}
for the standard differential operators acting upon maps
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle n\geq 1
.
As above with
\displaystyle u=(u^{1},\ldots,u^{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we use superscripts to specify co-ordinates of a vector when needed.
For sufficiently smooth functions

\displaystyle f=f(x)=f(x_{R},x_{S}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
we define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{V}_{0}f=\hat{V}_{0}f(x,\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\big\langle V_{0},\partial_{x}f\big\rangle+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}^{\top}\big(\partial_{x_{R}}^{2}f\big)V_{R,k};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{V}_{k}f=\hat{V}_{k}f(x,\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\big\langle V_{k},\partial_{x}f\big\rangle=\big\langle V_{R,k},\partial_{x_{R}}f\big\rangle,\qquad 1\leq k\leq d_{R}.

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{0}
,
\displaystyle\hat{V}_{k}
apply to vector-valued functions by separate consideration of the scalar co-ordinates.
Note that the original SDE (
1.1
) is equivalently given as the Stratonovitch-type SDE with drift function defined as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{0}(x,\theta)=V_{0}(x,\theta)-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}(x,\theta).

(1.4)

We define the vector-valued function:

[V_{k},V_{l}]=\hat{V}_{k}V_{l}(x,\theta)-\hat{V}_{l}V_{k}(x,\theta),\quad 1\leq k,l\leq d_{R}.

(1.5)

We write
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}=(\beta^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\in\Theta
for the (assumed unique) true value of
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)
.
We denote iterated stochastic integrals w.r.t. Brownian paths as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{\alpha}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t_{2}}dB_{\alpha_{1},t_{1}}\cdots dB_{\alpha_{l},t_{l}},\quad t>0,\ \ \alpha\in\{0,1,\ldots,d_{R}\}^{l},\ l\geq 0.

(1.6)

We will sometimes write
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
,
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}
to emphasise the involvement of
\displaystyle\theta
in calculations. Similarly, we will write
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}
,
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}

to stress, when needed, that derivations are under the true parameter value,
and write

\displaystyle\textstyle{\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}},\;\xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}}

to express convergence in probability and in distribution, respectively, under
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}
.
For a multi-index
\displaystyle\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{l},\,l\geq 1

and a sufficiently smooth
\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
we write

\displaystyle\textstyle{\partial^{\xi}_{\alpha}f(\xi):={\partial^{l}f(\xi)}/{\partial\xi_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial\xi_{\alpha_{l}}}},\;\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

2
Sampling schemes for elliptic & hypo-elliptic diffusions

We propose explicit sampling schemes for the SDE (
1.1
).
We introduce the conditions so that the law of
\displaystyle X_{t}
,
\displaystyle t>0
, admits a smooth Lebesgue density in Section
2.1
. Then, we present the sampling schemes in Section
2.2
. Hereafter, we make use of the notation
\displaystyle X_{t}^{x}
,
\displaystyle t>0
, when needed to emphasise the initial state
\displaystyle X_{0}=x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

2.1
Basic assumptions for diffusion class

We introduce basic conditions to characterise the SDE (
1.1
) we consider in this work.

(H1)

\displaystyle\Theta
is a compact subset of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
. For each
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and any multi-index
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{l}
,
\displaystyle l\geq 0
,
the function

\displaystyle\theta\mapsto\partial^{x}_{\alpha}V_{j}^{i}(x,\theta),\;0\leq j\leq{d_{R}},\;1\leq i\leq d,

is continuous.

(H2)

For each
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
,
\displaystyle V_{j}(\cdot,\theta)\in C_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\displaystyle 0\leq j\leq d_{R}
.

(H3)

The matrix
\displaystyle a_{R}=a_{R}(x;\sigma)
defined in (
A.1
) is positive-definite for all
\displaystyle(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
.

(H4)

For any
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
and any
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the set of the
\displaystyle 2d_{R}
vectors:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big\{V_{1}(x,\theta),\ldots,V_{d_{R}}(x,\theta),[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{1}](x,\theta),\ldots,[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{d_{R}}](x,\theta)\big\}\vskip-5.69046pt

spans
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
where
\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{0}=\widetilde{V}_{0}(x,\theta)
is defined in (
1.4
) and

\displaystyle[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{j}](x,\theta)
is defined as (
1.5
).

(
H4
) is relevant for hypo-elliptic SDEs and is stronger than Hörmander’s condition
(
Nualart 2006
)
, thus the law of
\displaystyle X_{t}^{x}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure for any
\displaystyle t>0
,
\displaystyle(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
. Hörmander’s condition allows for iterated Lie brackets of order larger than one (used here), e.g. of order two
\displaystyle[\widetilde{V}_{0},[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{k}]]
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq d_{R}
, or above,
to obtain vectors spanning
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We require (
H4
) so that certain discretisation schemes arising in our methodology also have a Lebesgue density.
Note that (
H2
), (
H4
) combined imply that
the density of
\displaystyle X_{t}^{x}
,
\displaystyle t>0
,
is infinitely differentiable
(
Nualart 2006
, Theorems 2.3.2, 2.3.3)
.

2.2
Approximate sampling scheme

We propose approximate sampling schemes for elliptic/hypo-elliptic SDE (
1.1
) that satisfy the following three Criteria:

(i)

The scheme is explicit.

(ii)

The scheme has local weak third order accuracy, i.e. for test functions
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
there exists a constant
\displaystyle C=C(x,\theta)>0
such that:

\big|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(X_{\Delta}^{x})]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x})]\big|\leq C\Delta^{3},\quad\Delta\geq 0,

where
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
denotes the approximated SDE position after a single step of size
\displaystyle\Delta>0
with an initial value
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{0}^{x}=x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(iii)

The distribution of
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
admits a Lebesgue density.

Criterion (ii) implies that the sampling scheme is a weak second order approximation of
\displaystyle\{X_{t}\}
in the following sense. Let
\displaystyle T>0
,
\displaystyle n\geq 1
, and consider the partition
\displaystyle 0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n-1}<t_{n}=T
with
\displaystyle t_{i}-t_{i-1}=T/n
,
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n
. Then, for
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
, there exists a constant
\displaystyle C=C(x,T,\theta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(X_{T}^{x})]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(\bar{X}_{T}^{(n),x})]\big|\leq{C}/{n^{2}},

where
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{T}^{(n),x}
is obtained after
\displaystyle n
iterations of a single step of the scheme, starting from the initial state
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Criterion (iii) is required so that a well-defined contrast function can be obtained and estimates of the parameters be produced.

2.2.1
Sampling scheme for elliptic diffusions

Before writing down our scheme satisfying Criteria (i)-(iii) above for elliptic diffusions, we present the ideas underlying the construction of a weak second order discretisation scheme (equivalently, a local weak third order scheme) following the moment matching techniques in
(
Milstein and Tretyakov 2021
, Section 2.1.2)
.
We apply an Itô-Taylor expansion
(
Kloeden and Platen 1992
)
for SDE model (
1.1
) with
\displaystyle d_{S}=0
and obtain that
\displaystyle X_{e,\Delta}^{x}=\hat{X}_{e,\Delta}^{x}+\rho_{e,\Delta}(x,\theta)
, where

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hat{X}_{e,\Delta}^{x}=&\;x+V_{R,0}(x,\theta)\Delta+\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)B_{k,\Delta}+\sum_{0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}{V}_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta),\end{aligned}

(2.1)

and
\displaystyle\rho_{e,\Delta}(x,\theta)
is a residual term involving stochastic iterated integrals of order three and above. Then, it can be shown
(
Milstein and Tretyakov 2021
)
that for test functions
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}};\mathbb{R})
,
\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
has local weak third order accuracy, though it is not an explicit scheme due to the presence of
\displaystyle I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta)
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}
,
\displaystyle k_{1}\neq k_{2}
, in its expression. Thus, we aim at replacing
\displaystyle I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta)
in (
2.1
) by some tractable random variables
\displaystyle\{\xi_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}\}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
so that a scheme using
\displaystyle\xi_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}
still achieves local weak third order convergence. Lemma 2.1.5 in
Milstein and Tretyakov 2021
states
the local weak third order accuracy is preserved if the random variables
\displaystyle\{\xi_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}\}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
have finite moments up to 6th order and satisfy a collection of moment conditions.

Thus, we write the sampling scheme for elliptic diffusions as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{X}_{e,\Delta}^{x}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;x+V_{R,0}(x,\theta)\Delta+\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)B_{k,\Delta}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq{d_{R}}\end{subarray}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)\,\xi_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta},

(2.2)

with the following specification of the random variables
\displaystyle\xi_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}
,
\displaystyle 0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}
, satisfying the moment conditions given in Lemma 2.1.5
Milstein and Tretyakov 2021
: for
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\xi_{00,\Delta}=\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2},\qquad\xi_{k_{1}0,\Delta}=\xi_{0k_{1},\Delta}=\tfrac{1}{2}B_{{k_{1}},\Delta}\Delta,\qquad\xi_{k_{1}k_{1},\Delta}=\tfrac{1}{2}B_{k_{1},\Delta}B_{k_{1},\Delta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta;\\
&\qquad\xi_{k_{1}k_{2},t}=\tfrac{1}{2}B_{k_{1},\Delta}B_{k_{2},\Delta}+\tfrac{1}{2}B_{k_{1},\Delta}\widetilde{B}_{k_{2},\Delta}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}<k_{2}}-\tfrac{1}{2}B_{k_{2},\Delta}\widetilde{B}_{k_{1},\Delta}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}>k_{2}}.\end{aligned}

(2.3)

Here,
\displaystyle\widetilde{B}:=(\widetilde{B}_{2,t},\ldots,\widetilde{B}_{d_{R},t})_{t\geq 0}
denotes a
\displaystyle(d_{R}-1)
-dimensional standard Brownian motion, independent of
\displaystyle B
.
Our scheme is marginally different from the one appearing in
Milstein and Tretyakov 2021
, with the former scheme using
\displaystyle(2d_{R}-1)
Gaussian variates, rather than
\displaystyle 2d_{R}
ones involved in the latter. The scheme is explicit as it only involves Gaussian variates – thus Criterion (i) stated above is satisfied.
Criterion (ii) is satisfied, given the above discussion. Criterion (iii) is clearly satisfied under (
H3
).

Remark 2.1
.

Scheme (
2.2
) under the choice of variates in (
2.3
) can also be used in a hypo-elliptic setting as an explicit weak second order scheme. However, such a specification
fails to meet Criterion (iii). Some more details are given in Remark
2.4
. Hence, in this work, we propose separate sampling schemes for the elliptic/hypo-elliptic classes.

In (
2.2
) we introduced the extra subscript ‘
\displaystyle e
’ for sampling scheme
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{e,\Delta}^{x}
to stress that it involves the elliptic case (not the hypo-elliptic one). We adopt a similar convention for mathematical expressions, when needed, in the rest of the paper.

2.2.2
Sampling scheme for hypo-elliptic diffusions

We treat hypo-elliptic SDEs, thus
\displaystyle d_{S}\geq 1
.
We make use of an Itô-Taylor expansion where for the rough component we retain all second order integrals and for the smooth one we retain the second order and third order integrals for which the outside integrator is
\displaystyle dt
.
That is, we have
\displaystyle X_{\Delta}^{x}=\hat{X}_{\Delta}^{x}+\rho_{\Delta}(x,\theta)
where:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{X}_{R,\Delta}^{x}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=x_{R}+V_{R,0}(x,\theta)\Delta+\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)\,B_{k,\Delta}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq{d_{R}}\end{subarray}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)\,I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{X}_{S,\Delta}^{x}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=x_{S}+V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,\Delta+\sum_{0\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,I_{(k,0)}(\Delta)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq{d_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,k_{1}=k_{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta).

As with the elliptic case, we next replace the non-explicit iterated integrals
\displaystyle I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta)
,
\displaystyle I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta)
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}
,
\displaystyle k_{1}\neq k_{2}
,
with explicit variates, based on moment conditions that ensure that the resulted scheme remains a weak second order one. The tools in
Milstein and Tretyakov 2021
cover only the setting of double integrals, thus we need to carry out an extension of such methodology in the presence of triple integrals.
The extension (see the proof of Proposition
2.1
stated below for details) gives rise to the sampling scheme

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}=[(\bar{X}_{R,\Delta}^{x})^{\top},(\bar{X}_{S,\Delta}^{x})^{\top}]^{\top}
,
\displaystyle x=[x_{R}^{\top},x_{S}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for hypo-elliptic SDEs determined as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{X}_{R,\Delta}^{x}&=x_{R}+V_{R,0}(x,\theta)\Delta+\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)\,B_{k,\Delta}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq{d_{R}}\end{subarray}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)\,\zeta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta};\\
\bar{X}_{S,\Delta}^{x}&=x_{S}+V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,\Delta+\sum_{0\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,\zeta_{k0,\Delta}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq{d_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,k_{1}=k_{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,\eta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}.\end{aligned}

(2.4)

Variables
\displaystyle\zeta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}
are such that
\displaystyle\zeta_{00,\Delta}={\Delta^{2}}/{2}
and for
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\zeta_{0k_{1},\Delta}=I_{(0,k_{1})}(\Delta),\quad\zeta_{k_{1}0,\Delta}=I_{(k_{1},0)}(\Delta),\quad\zeta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}=\xi_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta},

(2.5)

for
\displaystyle\xi
’s as determined earlier in (
2.3
) for the elliptic case.
Variables
\displaystyle\eta_{k_{1}k_{2},t}
are required to satisfy the following moment conditions, with
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}\leq{d_{R}}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=0,\quad\mathbb{E}[\eta_{k_{1}0,\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0,0)}(\Delta)]=0;

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta_{0k_{1},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(0,k_{1},0)}(\Delta)]=0,\quad\mathbb{E}[\eta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}B_{k_{3},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta)B_{k_{3},\Delta}]=0;

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta_{k_{1}0,\Delta}B_{k_{2},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[\eta_{0k_{1},\Delta}B_{k_{2},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0,0)}(\Delta)B_{k_{2},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(0,k_{1},0)}(\Delta)B_{k_{2},\Delta}]=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{6}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta_{k_{1}0,\Delta}\zeta_{k_{2}0,\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0,0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{4}}{8}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta_{0k_{1},\Delta}\zeta_{k_{2}0,\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(0,k_{1},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{4}}{6}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}\zeta_{k_{3}k_{4},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{3},k_{4})}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{6}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{3},k_{2}=k_{4}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}\eta_{k_{3}k_{4},\Delta}]=\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{3},k_{4},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{4}}{12}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{3},k_{2}=k_{4}}.

A particular choice for the
\displaystyle\eta
’s that we adopt for the rest of the paper is the one below:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta_{k_{1}0,\Delta}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{\Delta}{2}\zeta_{k_{1}0,\Delta}-\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{12}B_{k_{1},\Delta},\ \ \eta_{0k_{1},\Delta}=\Delta\zeta_{k_{1}0,\Delta}-\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{3}B_{k_{1},\Delta},\ \ \eta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}=\tfrac{1}{3}\zeta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}\Delta-\widetilde{\eta}_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}\Delta.

We have set:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{6\sqrt{2}}(\widetilde{B}_{k,\Delta}\widetilde{B}_{k,\Delta}-\Delta),&k_{1}=k_{2}=k;\\[5.69046pt]
\tfrac{1}{6\sqrt{2}}(\widetilde{B}_{k_{1},\Delta}\widetilde{B}_{k_{2},\Delta}+\widetilde{B}_{k_{1},\Delta}{W}_{k_{2},\Delta}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}<k_{2}}-\widetilde{B}_{k_{2},\Delta}{W}_{k_{1},\Delta}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}>k_{2}})&k_{1}\neq k_{2};\\[5.69046pt]
\end{cases}

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{B}:=(\widetilde{B}_{1,t},\ldots,\widetilde{B}_{{d_{R}},t})_{t\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle{W}=({W}_{2,t},\ldots,{W}_{{d_{R}},t})_{t\geq 0}
are standard Brownian motions, mutually independent and independent of
\displaystyle B
.

Remark 2.2
.

Generation of the Gaussian variables
\displaystyle\zeta_{0k,\Delta}
and
\displaystyle\zeta_{k0,\Delta}=B_{k,\Delta}\,\Delta-\zeta_{0k,\Delta}
in (
2.5
) must take under consideration the dependency structure amongst
\displaystyle\{B_{k,\Delta}\}
,
\displaystyle\{\zeta_{0k,\Delta}\}
. That is, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}\zeta_{0k_{2},\Delta}]={\Delta^{2}}/{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}},\ \mathbb{E}[\zeta_{0k_{1},\Delta}\zeta_{0k_{2},\Delta}]={\Delta^{3}}/{3}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}}

for
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}
. Thus, one can generate
\displaystyle\{B_{k,\Delta}\}
,

\displaystyle\{\zeta_{0k,\Delta}\}
and
\displaystyle\{\zeta_{k0,\Delta}\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{k,\Delta}=\Delta^{1/2}\,Z_{k},\quad\zeta_{0k,\Delta}{=}\tfrac{\Delta^{3/2}}{2}\big(Z_{k}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\widetilde{Z}_{k}\big),\quad\zeta_{k0,\Delta}{=}\Delta^{3/2}Z_{k}-\zeta_{0k,\Delta},\qquad 1\leq k\leq d_{R},

where
\displaystyle Z_{k},\widetilde{Z}_{k}\sim\mathscr{N}(0,1)
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq d_{R}
, are i.i.d. random variables.

We have developed scheme (
2.4
) that is explicit (Criterion (i)) and, as stated in the proposition below, it has local weak third order accuracy (Criterion (ii)), with the proof given in Section
D
of Supplementary Material. Criterion (iii) is discussed after the following proposition.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\displaystyle d_{S}\geq 1
and consider
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
. Under conditions (
H1
)–(
H2
), for any
\displaystyle(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
, there exist constants
\displaystyle C>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq 1
such that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(X_{\Delta}^{x})]-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x})]\big|\leq C(1+|x|^{q})\Delta^{3}.

Finally, w.r.t Criterion (iii) we notice that scheme (
2.4
) contains in its specification (i.e. once some terms are removed from (
2.4
)) the
local Gaussian scheme

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{LG},x}=\bigl[(\widetilde{X}_{R,\Delta}^{x})^{\,\top},(\widetilde{X}_{S,\Delta}^{x})^{\,\top}\bigr]^{\top}
given as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{X}_{\,R,\Delta}^{x}&=x_{R}+V_{R,0}(x,\beta)\Delta+\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)B_{k,\Delta};\\
\widetilde{X}_{S,\Delta}^{x}&=x_{S}+V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\Delta+\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}+\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)I_{(k,0)}(\Delta),\end{aligned}

(2.6)

where the covariance matrix is given as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x;\theta)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)^{\top}\Delta&\quad\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)^{\top}\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\\
\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)^{\top}\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}&\quad\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)^{\top}\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{3}\end{array}\right].

\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x;\theta)
is positive definite for any
\displaystyle(\Delta,x,\theta)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
under Assumptions (
H3
)–(
H4
), thus Criterion (iii) is satisfied.

Remark 2.3
.

Proposition
2.1
holds
for scheme (
2.4
) with or without retainment of the triple integral term in the smooth component

\displaystyle\textstyle I_{3}:=\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\{0,\ldots,{d_{R}}\}^{2}\setminus\{0,0\}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta).

However, our analysis of statistical methodology based on the above scheme will illustrate that inclusion of term
\displaystyle I_{3}
(or, more precisely, of an explicit substitute for
\displaystyle I_{3}
) is necessary for obtaining a CLT for the MLE in the complete observation regime under the improved rate
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
.

Remark 2.4
.

A difference versus the scheme for elliptic SDEs in (
2.2
) is that
in (
2.4
) variables

\displaystyle\textstyle{I_{(k,0)}(\Delta)}
,
\displaystyle\textstyle{I_{(0,k)}(\Delta)}
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq{d_{R}}
,
are kept and are not replaced by
\displaystyle\textstyle{\tfrac{1}{2}B_{k,\Delta}\Delta}
. A replacement of
\displaystyle\textstyle{I_{(k,0)}(\Delta)}
by
\displaystyle\textstyle{\tfrac{1}{2}B_{k,\Delta}\Delta}

would lead to a degenerate covariance matrix in place of
\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x;\theta)
and
a violation of Criterion (iii).

3
Small time density expansion

We denote the transition densities of the given SDE (
1.1
)
and the sampling scheme
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
by

y\mapsto p_{\Delta}^{X}(x,y;\theta)=\mathbb{P}_{\theta}[{X}_{\Delta}^{x}\in dy]/dy,\qquad y\mapsto p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)=\mathbb{P}_{\theta}[\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}\in dy]/dy

respectively, for
\displaystyle(\Delta,x,\theta)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
. The (one step) sampling scheme
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
in (
2.2
) or (
2.4
), for elliptic or hypo-elliptic SDEs respectively, does not admit, in general, a closed-form density function.
To obtain a proxy for the density, we work with a
small time expansion of the density of
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
given below in Lemma
3.1
.
The expansion will be called upon in Section
4
to provide a method for statistical inference that will possess advantageous characteristics compared with
existing methods that use constrast functions induced by conditionally Gaussian discretisation schemes, e.g. the local Gaussian scheme (
2.6
) in the hypo-elliptic case or the Euler-Maruyama scheme in the elliptic case.
We proceed by assuming a hypo-elliptic setting with
\displaystyle d_{S}\geq 1
, but our analysis also covers the elliptic case
\displaystyle d_{S}=0
.

3.1
Background material

Before giving the small time expansion formula for
\displaystyle p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
we sketch its derivation via use of Malliavin calculus, a differential calculus on Wiener space. Full rigorous arguments are given in Section
E
of Supplementary Material. We refer interested readers to, e.g.,
Watanabe 1987
;
Ikeda and Watanabe 2014
.

We consider Wiener functionals,
\displaystyle F=F(\omega):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
where for current Section
3.1
,
\displaystyle(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\},\mathbb{P})
is the probability space giving rise to the
\displaystyle 3\times d_{R}-1
Brownian motions involved in the definition of sampling scheme
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}
in (
2.4
).
Malliavin calculus allows for
\displaystyle\delta_{y}(F)
, with
\displaystyle\delta_{y}
the Dirac measure at
\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, to be well-defined as an element of a Sobolev space of Wiener functionals, provided that
\displaystyle F
satisfies regularity and non-degeneracy conditions, in which case such a Wiener functional is referred to as
smooth and non-degenerate in the Malliavin sense
. The non-degeneracy is a sufficient condition for the existence of Lebesgue density for the law of
\displaystyle F
, and in the case of a Gaussian variate, non-degeneracy is equivalent to the positive definiteness of the covariance matrix. It is shown that the Lebesgue density,
\displaystyle p^{F}(y)
, of
\displaystyle F
coincides with
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\,[\,\delta_{y}(F)\,]
, i.e. the
generalised expectation
of
\displaystyle\delta_{y}(F)
, if
\displaystyle F
is smooth and non-generate in the Malliavin sense.
We consider an
\displaystyle F=F^{\varepsilon}
, for small
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, of the form

\displaystyle\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}=f_{0}+\varepsilon f_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}f_{2}+\cdots+\varepsilon^{J}f_{J}

(3.1)

for Wiener functionals
\displaystyle f_{i}=f_{i}(\omega)
,
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq J
,
\displaystyle J\geq 1
.
We assume that
\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}
is smooth and non-degenerate in the Malliavin sense for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then, Theorem 9.4 in
Ikeda and Watanabe 2014
gives an asymptotic expansion of the density,
\displaystyle p^{F^{\varepsilon}}(y)
, of
\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}
as follows. For every
\displaystyle n\geq 0
, there exist Wiener functionals
\displaystyle\phi_{k}=\phi_{k}(\omega,y)
,
\displaystyle 0\leq k\leq n-1
, and
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
-valued
\displaystyle r_{n}(\cdot)
such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle p^{F^{\varepsilon}}(y)=\mathbb{E}\,[\,\delta_{y}(F^{\varepsilon})\,]=\sum_{0\leq k\leq n-1}\varepsilon^{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}\,[\,\phi_{k}(\cdot,y)\,]+\varepsilon^{n}\cdot r_{n}(y,\varepsilon),

(3.2)

with
\displaystyle|r_{n}(y,\varepsilon)|\leq C
for constant
\displaystyle C>0
independent of
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and the
\displaystyle\phi_{k}
’s are given via a formal Taylor expansion of
\displaystyle\varepsilon\to\delta_{y}(F^{\varepsilon})
, e.g.,
\displaystyle\phi_{0}=\delta_{y}(f_{0})
,
\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\langle\partial_{z}\delta_{y}(z)|_{z=f_{0}},f_{1}\rangle
.

3.2
Density expansion for sampling scheme

We develop a small time expansion for
the density
\displaystyle p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
of
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
in (
2.4
) via reference
to (
3.1
), (
3.2
). We make use of the multi-index notation:

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|:=l+(\mathrm{\#\;of\;zeros\;in\;}\alpha),\,|\alpha|:=l,

for
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{0,1,\ldots,d\}^{l},\,l\geq 0
.
Based on the expression for
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
in (
2.4
), we define

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon,x}=\big[\,(\bar{X}_{R,\Delta}^{\varepsilon,x})^{\top},\,(\bar{X}_{S,\Delta}^{\varepsilon,x})^{\top}\,\big]^{\top}
as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{X}_{R,\Delta}^{\varepsilon,x}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=x_{R}+\varepsilon^{2}V_{R,0}(x,\beta)\Delta+\varepsilon\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)\,B_{k,\Delta}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{\|(k_{1},k_{2})\|}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)\,\zeta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{X}_{S,\Delta}^{\varepsilon,x}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=x_{S}+\varepsilon^{2}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\,\Delta+\sum_{0\leq k\leq d_{R}}\varepsilon^{\|(k,0)\|}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,\zeta_{k0,\Delta}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq{d_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,k_{1}=k_{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{\|(k_{1},k_{2},0)\|}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\,\eta_{k_{1}k_{2},\Delta}.

The rationale in the consideration of the above process is that, first, the latter is connected with
scheme
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}
in (
2.4
)
via the equality in distributions

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\big[\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x}\in dy\big]=\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\big[\bar{X}_{1}^{\sqrt{\Delta},x}\in dy\big]

and, second, it is straightforward to perform the standardisation defined below upon process (
3.3
).
We cannot apply expansion (
3.2
) on the density of
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{1}^{\varepsilon,x}
as
\displaystyle\{\bar{X}_{1}^{\varepsilon,x}\}_{\varepsilon}
is degenerate in the Malliavin sense as
\displaystyle\varepsilon\to 0
, e.g. notice that
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{1}^{0,x}=x
is deterministic.
Instead, we introduce

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{\varepsilon}

via an appropriate standarisation of
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{1}^{\varepsilon,x}
based upon the local Gaussian scheme
\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{LG},x}=\bigl[(\widetilde{X}_{R,\Delta}^{x})^{\,\top},(\widetilde{X}_{S,\Delta}^{x})^{\,\top}\bigr]^{\top}
in (
2.6
). Thus, the small time expansion ultimately obtained will be centred around the density of the local Gaussian scheme.
In particular, we set

\displaystyle\displaystyle m_{x,\theta,\varepsilon}(y):=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}m_{R,x,\theta,\varepsilon}(y_{R})\\
m_{S,x,\theta,\varepsilon}(y_{S})\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{{y_{R}-x_{R}-\varepsilon^{2}V_{R,0}(x,\beta)}}{\varepsilon}\\
\frac{y_{S}-x_{S}-\varepsilon^{2}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)}{\varepsilon^{3}}\end{array}\right],

and, define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{\varepsilon}=\bigl[\bigl(\widetilde{Y}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{\top},\bigl(\widetilde{Y}_{S}^{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{\top}\bigr]^{\top}=m_{x,\theta,\varepsilon}(\bar{X}^{\varepsilon,x}_{1})=\widetilde{Y}^{(0)}+\sum_{1\leq l\leq 3}\varepsilon^{l}\cdot\widetilde{Y}^{(l)},

(3.8)

where one can obtain the
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(1)
-term in (
3.8
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{0}=[(\widetilde{Y}_{R}^{(0)})^{\top},(\widetilde{Y}_{S}^{(0)})^{\top}]^{\top}
, as

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{R}^{(0)}=\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)B_{k,1},\qquad\widetilde{Y}_{S}^{(0)}=\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)I_{(k,0)}(1),

with the remaining terms

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{(l)}
,
\displaystyle 1\leq l\leq 3
,
explicitly derived via (
3.3
)–(
3.8
).

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{(0)}
follows a Gaussian distribution and is non-degenerate under
(
H3
)–(
H4
)
, as the covariance matrix
\displaystyle\Sigma(1,x;\theta)
in (
2.2.2
) is positive definite. In brief, the above lead to non-degeneracy in the Malliavin sense of
\displaystyle\{\widetilde{Y}^{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon}
, uniformly in
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
Simple change of variables with
\displaystyle\varepsilon=\sqrt{\Delta}
yields:

\displaystyle\displaystyle p_{\Delta}^{\bar{X}}(x,y;\theta)=\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\big[\bar{X}_{1}^{\sqrt{\Delta},x}\in dy\big]/dy=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta^{d_{R}+3d_{S}}}}\,p^{\widetilde{Y}^{{\sqrt{\Delta}}}}(\xi;\theta)|_{\xi=m_{x,\theta,{\sqrt{\Delta}}}(y)}.

(3.9)

Thus, application of expansion (
3.2
) for the density of
\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{\varepsilon}
,
\displaystyle p^{\widetilde{Y}^{\varepsilon}}(\xi;\theta)=\mathbb{E}\,[\,\delta_{\xi}(\widetilde{Y}^{\varepsilon})\,]
, produces the small time expansion of
\displaystyle p_{\Delta}^{\bar{X}}(x,y;\theta)
.
Due to starting off from (
3.2
)
the rightmost side of (
3.9
) expands around the density of the local Gaussian scheme (
2.6
), that is:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta^{d_{R}+3d_{S}}}}\times p^{\widetilde{Y}^{(0)}}(\xi;\theta)\big|_{\xi=m_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y)}=p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta),

(3.10)

where
\displaystyle\xi\mapsto p^{\widetilde{Y}^{(0)}}(\xi;\theta)
denotes the density of the probability law of
\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{(0)}
and

\displaystyle y\mapsto p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
denotes the density of the local Gaussian scheme

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{LG},x}

defined in (
2.6
) and given as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\Sigma(\Delta,x;\theta)|}}\exp\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(y-\mu(\Delta,x;\theta))^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\Delta,x;\theta)(y-\mu(\Delta,x;\theta)\big)\Big),

(3.11)

for mean vector:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mu(\Delta,x;\theta)=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mu_{R}(\Delta,x;\beta)\\
\mu_{S}(\Delta,x;\theta)\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}x_{R}+V_{R,0}(x,\beta)\Delta\\
x_{S}+V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\Delta+\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\end{array}\right],

and covariance matrix
\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x;\theta)
given in (
2.2.2
).

To state the asymptotic expansion of
\displaystyle p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
, we introduce a class of Hermite polynomials based on the density of
\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}^{(0)}
. To simplify the notation we henceforth write
\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}=\widetilde{Y}^{(0)}
. Thus, we define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta):=(-1)^{{|\alpha|}}\,{\partial^{\xi}_{\alpha}\,p^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta)}/{p^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta)},\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\theta\in\Theta.

(3.16)

Also, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta)|_{\xi=m_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y)},\,(\Delta,x,y,\theta)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta.

We then have the following result whose proof is provided in Section
E
of Supplementary Material.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, and assume that conditions
(
H1
)–(
H4
) hold. Then,
for any integer
\displaystyle J\geq 4
, the transition density
\displaystyle y\mapsto p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
admits the following representation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)=p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\Bigl\{1+\sum_{1\leq l\leq J-1}\Delta^{{l}/{2}}\,\Psi_{l}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\Bigr\}+\frac{\Delta^{{J}/{2}}}{\sqrt{\Delta^{d_{R}+3d_{S}}}}R^{J}(x,y;\theta),\end{aligned}

(3.17)

where

\displaystyle R^{J}(x,y;\theta)
is the residual term satisfying

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\theta\in\Theta}|R^{J}(x,y;\theta)|<C

for some constant
\displaystyle C>0
, and
\displaystyle\Psi_{l}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)
,
\displaystyle l\geq 1
, have the general form:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Psi_{l}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\sum_{1\leq k\leq\nu(l)}\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{k}}v_{\alpha}(x,\theta)\times\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(\Delta,x,y;\theta),

(3.18)

for some positive integer
\displaystyle\nu(l)
, where
\displaystyle v_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
is explicitly given as a summation of products of
\displaystyle V_{j}(x,\theta)
,
\displaystyle 0\leq j\leq d_{R}
, and their partial derivatives. For the first two
\displaystyle\Psi
-terms in the expansion, we get:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d_{R}\\
1\leq i_{1}\leq d\end{subarray}}\biggl\{\hat{V}_{j_{1}}V_{j_{2}}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{R}}+\tfrac{1}{3}\hat{V}_{j_{1}}\hat{V}_{j_{2}}V_{0}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d_{R}+1\leq i_{1}\leq d}\biggr\}\cdot\widetilde{\Psi}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}j_{2}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta),

with

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}j_{2}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i_{2},i_{3}\leq d_{R}}V_{j_{1}}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)V_{j_{2}}^{i_{3}}(x,\theta)\,\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{R}+1\leq i_{2}\leq d\\
1\leq i_{3}\leq d_{R}\end{subarray}}\Bigl\{\tfrac{1}{3}\hat{V}_{j_{1}}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)V_{j_{2}}^{i_{3}}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{6}V_{j_{1}}^{i_{3}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{j_{2}}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\Bigr\}\,\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{8}\sum_{d_{R}+1\leq i_{2},i_{3}\leq d}\hat{V}_{j_{1}}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{j_{2}}V_{0}^{i_{3}}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta).

Also,

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\Phi_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

with

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d_{R}}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\Bigl(\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)V_{k}^{i_{2}}(x,\sigma)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d_{R}}\sum_{1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{R}\\
d_{R}+1\leq i_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{3}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{R}\\
d_{R}+1\leq i_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}V_{k}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\Bigl(\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{R}\\
d_{R}+1\leq i_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}\sum_{1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{d_{R}+1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{8}\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{24}\sum_{d_{R}+1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d}\sum_{1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta);

(3.19)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

involves higher order Hermite polynomials and is given in the form of

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{l=4,6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{l}\end{subarray}}w_{\alpha}(x,\theta)\times\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}(\Delta,x,y;\theta),

where
\displaystyle w_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
is of the same structure as the one described above for
\displaystyle v_{\alpha}
.

Remark 3.1
.

The Hermite polynomials have explicit expressions. E.g., for
\displaystyle 1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1})}^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta)|_{\xi=m_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y)}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq d}{\Sigma}_{i_{1}i}^{-1}(1,x;\theta)m^{i}_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta)|_{\xi=m_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y)}=\big\{\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1})}^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{2})}^{\widetilde{Y}}(\xi;\theta)\big\}|_{\xi=m_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y)}-{\Sigma}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{-1}(1,x;\theta).

(3.20)

Remark 3.2
.

We compare our small time density expansion (
3.17
) with some closed-form density expansions that have appeared in the literature.

(i)

Aït-Sahalia 2002
;
Aït-Sahalia 2008
provides Hermite series expansions for the density
\displaystyle p^{X}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
of elliptic diffusions.
However, the domains for
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
should be restricted to a compact set so that the related remainder term is bounded and convergence to the true density can be justified.

(ii)

Li 2013
develops a closed-form expansion formula for
\displaystyle p^{X}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
in the multivariate elliptic case using Malliavin-Watanabe calculus that enables one to obtain a remainder term that is uniformly bounded without imposing strong restrictions on the domain of
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Our approach also uses Malliavin-Watanabe calculus to obtain a closed-form expansion for

\displaystyle\textstyle{p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)}

with controllable remainder terms in a hypo-elliptic setting.

4
Analytic results for statistical inference

Making use of the small time density expansion
in Lemma
3.1
,
we provide analytic results about statistical inference procedures under high and low-frequency observation regimes.
We do not require all correction terms
\displaystyle\textstyle{\Delta^{{l}/{2}}\Psi_{l}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)}
,
\displaystyle l=1,\ldots
, appearing in the density expansion, and aim at obtaining statistical benefits with use of such terms when necessary.
Our results below identify the parts of the expansion that lead to parameter estimates of improved performance, for each of the two observation regimes.
Interestingly, such parts differ between the two regimes.

4.1
High-frequency observation regime

4.1.1
Contrast estimator

We consider the complete observation regime (
1.2
). Such a data setting has been studied in the recent works focused on hypo-elliptic SDEs of
Ditlevsen and Samson 2019
;
Gloter and Yoshida 2021
, and in numerous earlier studies for the elliptic case, see e.g.
Kessler 1997
;
Uchida and Yoshida 2012
.We introduce the following notation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}(x;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\Sigma(1,x;\theta),

(4.1)

where recall that
\displaystyle\Sigma
is the covariance matrix of local Gaussian scheme defined in (
2.2.2
). We consider the likelihood of the complete data in (
1.2
).
We work with the following proxy of
\displaystyle-2\times\textrm{log-likelihood}
, with

\displaystyle\mu=\mu(\Delta,x;\theta)
,
\displaystyle\Sigma=\Sigma(\Delta,x;\gamma,\sigma)
,
\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma_{1}(x;\gamma,\sigma)
defined in (
3.2
), (
2.2.2
), (
4.1
), respectively:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)&:=\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\big(X_{t_{m}}-\mu(\Delta,X_{t_{m-1}};\theta)\big)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\Delta,X_{t_{m-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\big(X_{t_{m}}-\mu(\Delta,X_{t_{m-1}};\theta)\big)\\
&\qquad+\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\log|\Sigma_{1}(X_{t_{m-1}};\gamma,\sigma)|-2\Delta\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\Phi_{2}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta),\end{aligned}

(4.2)

where
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
is given in (
3.1
), namely,
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
is a term with second order Hermite polynomials appearing in the expression for
\displaystyle\Psi_{2}
in the density expansion (
3.17
).
We discuss the effect of the correction term
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
in the obtained CLT in Remark
4.2
later in the paper. In the elliptic case, i.e.
\displaystyle d=d_{R}
,
\displaystyle d_{\gamma}=0
, the corresponding contrast function is as in (
4.2
) but with functions
\displaystyle\mu
,
\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}
,
\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}
,
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
replaced by
\displaystyle\mu_{R}
,
\displaystyle a_{R}
,
\displaystyle a_{R}^{-1}/\Delta
,
\displaystyle\Phi_{e,2}
, respectively.
We define the contrast estimator

\displaystyle\textstyle{\hat{\theta}_{n}:=(\hat{\beta}_{n},\hat{\gamma}_{n},\hat{\sigma}_{n})=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)}

and write the estimator as

\displaystyle\textstyle{\hat{\theta}_{e,n}=(\hat{\beta}_{n},\hat{\sigma}_{n})}
in the setting of elliptic diffusions.

Remark 4.1
.

We state a property of term
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
defined in (
3.1
). For simplicity, we write

\displaystyle\textstyle\Phi_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\sum_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d}G_{i_{1}i_{2}}(x;\theta)\times\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta),\,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

for appropriate functions
\displaystyle G_{i_{1}i_{2}}
obtain via (
3.1
).
Terms
\displaystyle G_{i_{1}i_{2}}(x;\theta)
also arise when considering the covariance of the normalised vector

\displaystyle m(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=m_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y)
.
That is, one obtains: for
\displaystyle 1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl[m^{i_{1}}(\Delta,x,X_{\Delta}^{x};\theta)\,m^{i_{2}}(\Delta,x,X_{\Delta}^{x};\theta)\bigr]=\Sigma_{1,i_{1}i_{2}}(x;\theta)+2\Delta\times G_{i_{1}i_{2}}(x;\theta)+\widetilde{G}_{i_{1}i_{2}}(\Delta^{2},x;\theta),

(4.3)

for
\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{i_{1}i_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta):[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle|\widetilde{G}_{i_{1}i_{2}}(h,x;\theta)|\leq Ch
for some
\displaystyle C>0
, and
\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{i_{1}i_{2}}(h,\cdot;\theta)\in C_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
for all
\displaystyle(h,\theta)\in[0,\infty)\times\Theta
under (
H1
)–(
H2
).
The above connection between
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
and the covariance expression (
4.3
) plays a key role to obtain a CLT under the weaker condition
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
.

4.1.2
Conditions for high-frequency regime

To state the main result, we introduce the following additional conditions.

(H5)

For each
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle\theta\mapsto V_{j}(x,\theta)
is three times differentiable.
For any
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,d_{\theta}\}^{l}
, with
\displaystyle l\in\{1,2\}
, the functions

\displaystyle x\mapsto\partial^{\theta}_{\alpha}V_{j}^{i}(x,\theta),\;0\leq j\leq d_{R},\;1\leq i\leq d,

have bounded derivatives of every order uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
.

(H6)

For
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq d_{S}
, there exists a function

\displaystyle B^{i}\in C_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})

such that for any
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\in\Theta_{\gamma}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle|V_{S,0}^{i}(x,\gamma^{\prime})-V_{S,0}^{i}(x,\gamma)|\leq B^{i}(x)|\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma|.

(H7)

The diffusion process
\displaystyle\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in (
1.1
) is ergodic under
\displaystyle\theta=\theta^{\dagger}
, with invariant distribution
\displaystyle\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Furthermore, all moments of
\displaystyle\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}
are finite.

(H8)

It holds that for all
\displaystyle p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{t>0}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[|X_{t}|^{p}]<\infty
.

(H9)

The true parameters lie in the interior of
\displaystyle\Theta
. If it holds

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{R,0}(x,\beta)=V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger}),\ \ V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)=V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger}),\ \ V(x,\sigma)=V(x,\sigma^{\dagger}),

for
\displaystyle x
in a set of probability 1 under
\displaystyle\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}
, then

\displaystyle\beta=\beta^{\dagger},\,\gamma=\gamma^{\dagger},\,\sigma=\sigma^{\dagger}
.

4.1.3
Asymptotic properties of the contrast estimator

We can now prove that the estimator
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}
has the following asymptotic properties.

Theorem 4.1

(Consistency)
.

Under conditions (
H1
)–(
H9
), it holds that if
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
, then

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\theta^{\dagger}.

Theorem 4.2

(Asymptotic normality)
.

Under conditions (
H1
)–(
H9
), it holds that if
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
and
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
, then:

(a)

if
\displaystyle d=d_{R}
, i.e. in the setting of elliptic diffusions,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Bigl[\sqrt{n\Delta}\,(\hat{\beta}_{n}-\beta^{\dagger})^{\top},\sqrt{n}\,(\hat{\sigma}_{n}-\sigma^{\dagger})^{\top}\Bigr]^{\top}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\mathscr{N}\bigl(0,I_{e}(\theta^{\dagger})^{-1}\bigr),

(b)

if
\displaystyle d>d_{R}
, i.e. in the setting of hypo-elliptic diffusions,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Bigl[\sqrt{n\Delta}\,(\hat{\beta}_{n}-\beta^{\dagger})^{\top},\,\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\Delta}}\,(\hat{\gamma}_{n}-\gamma^{\dagger})^{\top},\,\sqrt{n}\,(\hat{\sigma}_{n}-\sigma^{\dagger})^{\top}\Bigr]^{\top}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\mathscr{N}\bigl(0,I(\theta^{\dagger})^{-1}\bigr).

\displaystyle I_{e}(\theta^{\dagger})=\mathrm{diag}\bigl[\big(I_{e,ij}^{\,\beta}(\theta^{\dagger})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq d_{\beta}},\bigl(I_{e,ij}^{\,\sigma}(\theta^{\dagger})\bigr)_{1\leq i,j\leq d_{\sigma}}\bigr]
is the asymptotic precision matrix
with block matrix elements:

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{e,ij}^{\,\beta}(\theta^{\dagger})=\int\big(\,\partial_{\beta_{i}}V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger})^{\top}a_{R}^{-1}(x,\sigma^{\dagger})\,\partial_{\beta_{j}}V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger})\,\big)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{e,ij}^{\sigma}(\theta^{\dagger})=\tfrac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\,\partial_{\sigma_{i}}a_{R}(x,\sigma^{\dagger})\,a_{R}^{-1}(x,\sigma^{\dagger})\,\partial_{\sigma_{j}}a_{R}(x,\sigma^{\dagger})\,a_{R}^{-1}(x,\sigma^{\dagger})\,\bigr)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx).

Similarly, the asymptotic precision matrix
\displaystyle I(\theta^{\dagger})
has the block-diagonal structure:

\displaystyle\displaystyle I(\theta^{\dagger})=\mathrm{diag}\bigl[\big(I_{ij}^{\,\beta}(\theta^{\dagger})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq d_{\beta}},\big(I_{ij}^{\,\gamma}(\theta^{\dagger})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq d_{\gamma}},\bigl(I_{ij}^{\,\sigma}(\theta^{\dagger})\bigr)_{1\leq i,j\leq d_{\sigma}}\bigr],

(4.4)

with block matrix elements:

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{ij}^{\,\beta}(\theta^{\dagger})=\int\big(\,\partial_{\beta_{i}}V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger})^{\top}{\Sigma}_{1,RR}^{-1}(x;\sigma^{\dagger})\,\partial_{\beta_{j}}V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger})\,\big)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{ij}^{\,\gamma}(\theta^{\dagger})=4\int\big(\,\partial_{\gamma_{i}}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})^{\top}\,{\Sigma}_{1,SS}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\,\partial_{\gamma_{j}}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})\,\big)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{ij}^{\sigma}(\theta^{\dagger})=\tfrac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\,\partial_{\sigma_{i}}{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\,{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\,\partial_{\sigma_{j}}{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\,{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\,\bigr)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx).

The proofs of Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
are given in Sections
B.1
and
B.2
, respectively, in Supplementary Material.

Remark 4.2
.

Gloter and Yoshida 2021
obtain an MLE using the transition density of the local Gaussian scheme in (
2.6
), that is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}_{n}=\mathrm{argmin}_{\theta\in\Theta}\widetilde{\ell}_{n,\Delta}(\theta)

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\ell}_{n,\Delta}(\theta)
is given by (
4.2
) without the last term
\displaystyle\textstyle-2\Delta\sum_{m=1}^{n}\Phi_{2}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta)
.
They prove asymptotic normality for
\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
under the conditions
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
and
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
.
The asymptotic precision they obtain is identical to the one we find here in (
4.4
). Thus, addition of the term
\displaystyle\textstyle{-2\Delta\sum_{m=1}^{n}\Phi_{2}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta})
allows for a weaker experimental design condition under which a CLT holds, even if it does not alter the asymptotic variance.

Remark 4.3
.

We explain that the proposed estimator
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}
contributes to an improvement mainly in the estimation of diffusion parameter
\displaystyle\sigma
when compared with the existing estimator
\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
. This is clarified by observing the role of the weaker condition
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
for the CLT.
To prove the CLT, we show:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[s(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta^{\dagger})|\mathcal{F}_{t_{m-1}}]\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,

(4.5)

as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
, with the aid of
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
, where
\displaystyle s=s(\cdot;\cdot)
is determined from the scaled score function as:

\bigl[\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}}}\partial_{\beta}^{\top}\,\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta^{\dagger}),\,\sqrt{\tfrac{\Delta_{n}}{n}}\partial_{\gamma}^{\top}\,\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta^{\dagger}),\,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\partial_{\sigma}\,\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta^{\dagger})\bigr]^{\top}\equiv\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}s(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta^{\dagger}).

Since the contrast function (
4.2
) splits into a log-Gaussian part (related to the local Gaussian scheme) and a correction part (involving
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
), we write the score function as:

\displaystyle s(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger})\equiv s_{\mathrm{LG}}(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger})+s_{\Phi_{2}}(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger}).

Then, the expansion formula (
4.3
) yields:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[{s}^{\,i_{1}}_{\mathrm{LG}}(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta^{\dagger})|\mathcal{F}_{t_{m-1}}]=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}{g}^{i_{1}}\bigl(\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{3}},{X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger}\bigr),\quad 1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[{s}^{\,i_{2}}_{\mathrm{LG}}(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta^{\dagger})|\mathcal{F}_{t_{m-1}}]=\tfrac{\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{2}}}{n}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\mathrm{tr}\bigl(2\cdot G({X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger})\partial_{\theta_{i_{2}}}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}({X}_{t_{m-1}};\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}{g}^{i_{2}}\bigl(\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{4}},{X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger}\bigr),\quad d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma}+1\leq i_{2}\leq d_{\theta},

(4.6)

where
\displaystyle g^{i}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta^{\dagger}):[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},\,1\leq i\leq d,

has the same regularity property as
\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{i_{1}i_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta^{\dagger})

in (
4.3
). The above two equations correspond to the score functions of the drift parameters
\displaystyle(\beta,\gamma)\in\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\gamma}
and the diffusion parameter
\displaystyle\sigma\in\Theta_{\sigma}
, respectively. Also for the correction part, we show that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[{s}^{\,i_{1}}_{\Phi_{2}}(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta^{\dagger})|\mathcal{F}_{t_{m-1}}]=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\widetilde{g}^{i_{1}}\bigl(\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{3}},{X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger}\bigr),\quad 1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[{s}^{\,i_{2}}_{\Phi_{2}}(\Delta_{n},{X}_{t_{m-1}},{X}_{t_{m}};\theta^{\dagger})|\mathcal{F}_{t_{m-1}}]=-\tfrac{\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{2}}}{n}\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\mathrm{tr}\bigl(2\cdot G({X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger})\partial_{\theta_{i_{2}}}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}({X}_{t_{m-1}};\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\widetilde{g}^{i_{2}}\bigl(\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{4}},{X}_{t_{m-1}};\theta^{\dagger}\bigr),\quad d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma}+1\leq i_{2}\leq d_{\theta},

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{g}^{i}
has the same structure as
\displaystyle g^{i}
. Thus, inclusion of the
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
-term in our contrast function
\displaystyle\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)
in (
4.2
), results in the cancellation of a quantity corresponding to the 1st term of size
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{2}})}
on the right side of (
4.6
), and then convergence (
4.5
) holds under
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
. For details, see the proof of Lemma
B.5
in Section
C.3.1
in Supplementary Material. Note that the required condition for
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}
is weakened for the score w.r.t. diffusion parameter, while the condition for the score w.r.t. drift parameters remains the same after inclusion of the correction part (see
\displaystyle g^{i_{1}}
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{g}^{i_{1}}
).
Thus, it is expected that the new estimator performs better than the existing estimator
\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}_{n}
in the estimation of diffusion parameter.

4.1.4
Contrast estimator for stochastic damping Hamiltonian systems

We focus on an important sub-class of hypo-elliptic diffusions used in applications, namely the
stochastic damping Hamiltonian systems
, and write down in detail the form of the proposed contrast function for such a family of models.
An SDE in the above class can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dX_{R,t}&=-(c_{\beta}(X_{S,t})X_{R,t}+g_{\beta}(X_{S,t}))dt+\Xi(\sigma)\,dB_{t},\quad X_{R,0}=x_{R,0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\bar{d}};\\
dX_{S,t}&=X_{R,t}dt,\quad X_{S,0}=x_{S,0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\bar{d}},\end{aligned}

(4.7)

where
\displaystyle d_{S}=d_{R}=\bar{d}\geq 1
,
\displaystyle g_{\beta}:\mathbb{R}^{\bar{d}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\bar{d}}
,
\displaystyle c_{\beta}:\mathbb{R}^{\bar{d}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\bar{d}\times\bar{d}}
are smooth functions depending on some parameter
\displaystyle\beta\in\Theta_{\beta}
, and
\displaystyle\Xi(\sigma)=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma)
for
\displaystyle\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\bar{d}})\in\Theta_{\sigma}
with
\displaystyle\sigma_{j}>0,\,1\leq j\leq\bar{d}
.
System (
4.7
) includes many models used in applications, including, e.g., the Langevin equation
(
Pavliotis 2014
)
.

For
\displaystyle X_{t}=[X_{R,t}^{\top},X_{S,t}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\bar{d}}
given by (
4.7
), the local Gaussian density (
3.11
) is specified via the following mean vector
\displaystyle\mu(\Delta,x;\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2\bar{d}}
and covariance matrix
\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x;\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2\bar{d}\times 2\bar{d}}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mu(\Delta,x;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}x_{R}-(c_{\beta}(x_{S})x_{R}+g_{\beta}(x_{S}))\cdot\Delta\\
x_{S}+x_{R}\cdot\Delta-(c_{\beta}(x_{S})x_{R}+g_{\beta}(x_{S}))\cdot{\Delta^{2}}/{2}\end{bmatrix},\ \ \Sigma(\Delta,x;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\Delta&{\Delta^{2}}/{2}\\
{\Delta^{2}}/{2}&{\Delta^{3}}/{3}\end{bmatrix}\otimes\Xi(\sigma)\Xi(\sigma)^{\top}.

The diffusion matrix
\displaystyle\Xi
is independent of the state
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{2\bar{d}}
and the drift function is linear
in
\displaystyle X_{R,t}
given
\displaystyle X_{S,t}
. Thus, most of the terms in the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
appearing in the contrast (
4.2
) are equal to
\displaystyle 0
, e.g.,

\displaystyle\textstyle\hat{V}_{j_{1}}V_{k_{1}}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)=0,\ \hat{V}_{j_{2}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)=0
for

\displaystyle 1\leq i_{1},k_{1},k_{2}\leq\bar{d},\,0\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq\bar{d},\,\bar{d}+1\leq i_{2}\leq 2\bar{d}.

Thus, the correction term
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
in the case of system (
4.7
) takes up a simple form. For
\displaystyle x=(x_{R},x_{S}),y\in\mathbb{R}^{2\bar{d}},\Delta>0
and
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta
, we get:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\sum_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq\bar{d}}c_{\beta,i_{1}i_{2}}(x_{S})\times(\sigma_{i_{2}})^{2}\times\Bigl\{\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1}i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+\tfrac{1}{3}\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1}i_{2}+\bar{d})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{1}{6}\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1}+\bar{d},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+\tfrac{1}{8}\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1}+\bar{d},i_{2}+\bar{d})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\Bigr\},

(4.8)

where
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1}i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)
is the second order Hermite polynomial defined in (
3.20
).

4.2
Low-frequency observation regime

We consider the scenario of low-frequency observations, i.e. the time interval among successive observations,
\displaystyle\Delta=t_{i}-t_{i-1}
,
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n
, is now assumed fixed, and large enough so that approximation schemes must be combined with a
data augmentation
approach. Thus, we move onto a Bayesian inference setting.
Let
\displaystyle\delta=\delta_{M}:=\Delta/M,\;M\geq 1,

be the user-induced step-size after imputation of
\displaystyle(M-1)
data points amongst a pair of observations.
Given a transition density scheme, say

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{\delta}(x,y;\theta)
,
the true (intractable) transition density is approximated as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle p_{\Delta}^{X}(X_{t_{i-1}},X_{t_{i}};\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\approx\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d\times(M-1)}}\big\{\prod_{1\leq j\leq M}\bar{p}_{\delta}(x_{j-1},x_{j};\theta)\big\}\,dx_{1}\cdots dx_{M-1},\quad x_{0}={X}_{t_{i-1}},\;x_{M}={X}_{t_{i}}.

In the case of elliptic diffusions, i.e.,
\displaystyle d_{S}=0,\,d_{R}=d
,
Gobet and Labart 2008
showed that the bias induced by the Euler-Maruyama (EM) scheme is of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M^{-1})
.

In Section
4.2.1
,
4.2.2
we develop two-types of explicit transition density schemes achieving local weak third order convergence via appropriate choice of higher order correction terms in the density expansion formula (
3.17
).
We then illustrate in Section
4.2.3
that the discretisation bias of the developed transition schemes is of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M^{-2})
, for the class of elliptic SDEs.

4.2.1
Local weak third order transition density scheme – version I

Making use of the small time expansion of the transition density
\displaystyle y\mapsto p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
in Lemma
3.1
, we obtain
the following key result whose proof is given in Section
F
of Supplementary Material.

Proposition 4.1

(Density expansion for local third order weak approximation)
.

Let
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, and assume that conditions
(
H1
)–(
H4
) hold. Then, for any integer
\displaystyle J\geq 4
, the transition density
\displaystyle y\mapsto p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
, admits the following representation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} p^{\bar{X}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)=p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\bigl\{1+\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+R_{1}^{J}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\bigr\}+\frac{\Delta^{{J}/{2}}}{\sqrt{\Delta^{d_{R}+3d_{S}}}}R_{2}^{J}(x,y;\theta),\end{aligned}

with residual terms
\displaystyle R_{1}^{J}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)
and
\displaystyle R_{2}^{J}(x,y;\theta)
. In particular:

(i)

We have the expression:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)=\sqrt{\Delta}\cdot\Psi_{1}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+\Delta\cdot\Psi_{2}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+\sqrt{\Delta^{3}}\cdot\Psi_{3}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta),

(4.9)

for the individual terms:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\leq d_{R}}\sum_{1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}}\hskip-11.38092pt\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)V_{k_{1}}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)V_{k_{2}}^{i_{3}}(x,\theta)\,\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta);

(4.10)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d_{R}}\sum_{1\leq k\leq d_{R}}\Bigl(\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)V_{k}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d_{R}}\sum_{1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,x,y;\theta);

(4.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Psi_{3}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\leq d_{R}}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{i}(x,\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i)}(\Delta,x,y;\theta).

(4.12)

(ii)

For any
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
, there exist constants
\displaystyle C>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq 1
such that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Bigl|\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)p_{\Delta}^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}(x,y;\theta)R_{1}^{J}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)dy\,\Bigr|\leq C(1+|x|^{q})\Delta^{3}.

(4.13)

(iii)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\theta\in\Theta}}|R_{2}^{J}(x,y;\theta)|<C
, for a constant
\displaystyle C>0
.

(iv)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)dy=0}
.

Thus, from (ii) and (iii) with
\displaystyle J\geq 6
, we have that for any
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
and
\displaystyle(\Delta,x,\theta)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
, there exist constants
\displaystyle C>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq 1
such that:
