\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big|\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(\bar{X}_{\Delta}^{x})]-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\bigl\{1+\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\bigr\}dy\,\Big|\;\leq\;C(1+|x|^{q})\Delta^{3}.

Using Proposition
2.1
, the above gives immediately that, for any
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
and
\displaystyle(\Delta,x,\theta)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
, there exist constants
\displaystyle C>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq 1
so that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Big|\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(X_{\Delta}^{x})]-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\,p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\bigl\{1+\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\bigr\}dy\,\Big|\;\leq\;C(1+|x|^{q})\Delta^{3}.\end{aligned}

In the case of elliptic diffusions (
\displaystyle d=d_{R}
,
\displaystyle d_{S}=0
), Proposition
4.1
is interpreted as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} p^{\bar{X}}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta)=p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{EM}}}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\,\big\{1+\Psi_{e}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+R_{e,1}^{J}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\bigr\}+\frac{\Delta^{{J}/{2}}}{\sqrt{\Delta^{d_{R}}}}R_{e,2}^{J}(x,y;\theta),\end{aligned}

where
\displaystyle y\mapsto p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{EM}}}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta)

denotes the density of the one-step Euler-Maruyama scheme given
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
and the start point
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}}
, and terms
\displaystyle R_{e,1}^{J}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)
,
\displaystyle R_{e,2}^{J}(x,y;\theta)
have the same properties as the corresponding ones in Proposition
4.1
.
The correction part
\displaystyle\Psi_{e}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

corresponds to
\displaystyle\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)
with
\displaystyle d=d_{R}
and the Hermite polynomials replaced with
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}^{\widetilde{Y}_{e}}(\xi;\theta)}|_{\xi=\textstyle{m_{x,\theta,\sqrt{\Delta}}(y)}}
whose definition has been adjusted in an apparent way to conform to the setting
\displaystyle d_{S}=0
.

In light of Proposition
4.1
,
we propose the use of the following transition density scheme for the hypo-elliptic case:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta):=p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\big\{1+\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\big\},

(4.14)

for
\displaystyle\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)
as given in (
4.9
),
with the following corresponding scheme for the elliptic case:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I}}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta):=p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{EM}}}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\big\{1+\Psi_{e}^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\big\}.

(4.15)

It is worth mentioning that the quantity

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I}}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta)

has also appeared in results by
Iguchi and Yamada 2021a
;
Iguchi and Yamada 2022
, where a weak high order approximation was developed for elliptic SDEs via a different approach.
We also note that

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)

has the following properties:

(i)

The normalising constant is equal to 1, i.e. it holds that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\bigl\{1+\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\bigr\}dy=1.

(ii)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)
, thus, also,

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
, can, in general, take negative values, as do other closed-form expansions proposed by
Aït-Sahalia 2008
and
Li 2013
.

4.2.2
Local weak third order transition density scheme – version II

We construct an alternative transition density scheme that provides a proper, everywhere positive, density function. See also
Stramer et al. 2010
for a related consideration in the context of the density expansion for elliptic SDEs put forward by
Aït-Sahalia 2002
.
We consider the truncated Taylor expansion of
\displaystyle\log(1+z)
and introduce, for
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle K(z):=\sum_{1\leq l\leq 6}(-1)^{l+1}\tfrac{z^{l}}{l}.

(4.16)

For
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, we define the unnormalised non-negative approximation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta):=p^{\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{LG}}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\exp\bigl(K(\Psi^{\mathrm{weak}}(\Delta,x,y;\theta))\bigr).

(4.17)

The mapping
\displaystyle y\mapsto\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
is integrable as the highest order term of
\displaystyle K(z)
in (
4.16
) is
\displaystyle-z^{6}
, so
\displaystyle\exp(K(z))
is bounded. Notice that the normalising constant

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{Z}(\Delta,x;\theta):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)dy}

is no longer
\displaystyle 1
. Nevertheless, the unnormalised non-negative approximation

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)

delivers a local weak third order approximation for
\displaystyle X_{\Delta}^{x}
, in the following sense with its proof provided in Section
G
of Supplementary Material.

Proposition 4.2
.

For any
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
and
\displaystyle(\Delta,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
, there exist constants
\displaystyle C>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq 1
, such that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)dy\,\Big|\leq C(1+|x|^{q})\Delta^{3},

(4.18)

and then, from Proposition
4.1
(iv),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big|\,\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\varphi(X_{\Delta}^{x})]-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)dy\,\Big|\leq C(1+|x|^{q})\Delta^{3},

for some constants
\displaystyle C>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq 1
.

The transition density scheme
\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II}}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)
in (
4.17
) has the properties:

(i)

It takes non-negative values for all
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
.

(ii)

From Proposition
4.2
, it provides a local weak third order approximation for the solution of the SDE (
1.1
), for test functions
\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
. The discrepancy between the normalising constant
\displaystyle\bar{Z}(\Delta,x;\theta)
and
\displaystyle 1
is of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta^{3})
, by setting
\displaystyle\varphi(y)=1
in (
4.18
).

4.2.3
Discretisation based upon the transition density schemes

We finally illustrate the discretisation bias induced from the proposed transition density schemes for elliptic SDEs, i.e.,
\displaystyle d_{S}=0,d_{R}=d
. For the first-type transition density scheme in (
4.15
),
Iguchi and Yamada 2021a
showed that the discretisation bias is of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M^{-2})
, i.e., there exist constants
\displaystyle C,c>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq d_{R}/2
and a non-decreasing
\displaystyle h(\cdot)
such that for any
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big|\,p_{e,\Delta}^{X}(x,y;\theta)-\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{I},(M)}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\,\Big|\leq\frac{C}{M^{2}}\frac{h(\Delta)}{\Delta^{q}}e^{-c\,\tfrac{|y-x|^{2}}{\Delta}},

(4.19)

Remark 4.4
.

The bound (
4.19
) was derived without reference to unknown SDE parameters, thus are not uniform in
\displaystyle\theta
. Nevertheless, under compactness of
\displaystyle\Theta
, see (
H1
), they can be readily
adapted to be uniform also in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
.

We have the following estimate for the second-type transition density scheme, which is a main new result obtained in this section, with a proof given in Section
H
of Supplementary Material.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\displaystyle\Delta>0
. Assume
\displaystyle d_{S}=0
and that conditions (
H1
)–(
H3
) hold. Then, there exist constants
\displaystyle C,c>0
,
\displaystyle q\geq d_{R}/2
and a non-decreasing function
\displaystyle h(\cdot)
such that for any
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}}
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big|\,p_{e,\Delta}^{X}(x,y;\theta)-\bar{p}^{\,\mathrm{II},(M)}_{e,\Delta}(x,y;\theta)\,\Big|\leq\frac{C}{M^{2}}\frac{h(\Delta)}{\Delta^{q}}e^{-c\,\tfrac{|y-x|^{2}}{\Delta}}.

(4.20)

5
Numerical experiments

We provide numerical experiments related to the analytic results in Section
4
.
Note that our software implementation (
https://github.com/matt-graham/simsde
) of both the proposed contrast function and the sampling schemes is fully general.
The user is only required to specify functions for evaluating the drift and diffusion coefficient terms for given a process state and model parameters, with functions for evaluating the contrast estimator or simulating from the sampling scheme then automatically generated. This is achieved by symbolically computing the terms in the relevant estimator or sampling scheme expression using the Python-based computer algebra system SymPy (
https://www.sympy.org/
). The generated functions can also optionally use the numerical primitives defined in the high-performance numerical computing framework JAX (
https://jax.readthedocs.io/
); this allows the derivatives of the generated functions to be computed using JAX’s automatic differentiation support.
We exploit the above to automatically generate efficient functions for computing the gradient of the contrast function in the optimisation-based simulation study in Section
5.1
and the gradient of the posterior density in the Bayesian inference numerical experiments in Section
5.2
.

5.1
High-frequency observation regime

We consider the stochastic Jansen–Rit neural mass model that describes the evolution of neural population in a local cortical circuit, that is, the interaction of the main pyramidal cells with the excitatory and inhibitory interneurons. See
Ableidinger et al. 2017
for more details about the model. The model is defined as the following
\displaystyle 6
-dimensional hypo-elliptic SDE driven by a
\displaystyle 3
-dimensional Brownian motion (
\displaystyle d=6
,
\displaystyle d_{S}=d_{R}=3
):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dX_{R,t}&=\bigl(-\Gamma_{\theta}^{2}X_{S,t}-2\Gamma_{\theta}X_{R,t}+G(X_{S,t},\theta)\bigr)dt+\Sigma_{\theta}dB_{t},\quad X_{R,0}=x_{R,0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3};\\
dX_{S,t}&=X_{R,t}dt,\quad X_{S,0}=x_{S,0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{aligned}

(5.1)

for parameter specified as
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\sigma)
with

\displaystyle\beta=(A,B,C,\mu,\nu_{0},a,b,r,\nu_{\mathrm{max}})\in\mathbb{R}^{5}\times(0,\infty)^{4},\,\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})\in(0,\infty)^{3}.

We have set
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}:=\mathrm{diag}(a,a,b)
,
\displaystyle\Sigma_{\theta}:=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})
and

\displaystyle\displaystyle G(x_{S},\theta):=\bigl[Aa\times\mathcal{S}_{\theta}(x_{S}^{2}-x_{S}^{3}),\,Aa\bigl(\mu+C_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{\theta}(Cx_{S}^{1})\bigr),\,Bb\,C_{2}\times\mathcal{S}_{\theta}(C_{3}\,x_{S}^{1})\bigr]^{\top},\quad x_{S}=(x_{S}^{1},x_{S}^{2},x_{S}^{3}),

where
\displaystyle C_{1}=0.8\times C
,
\displaystyle C_{2}=C_{3}=0.25\times C
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\nu_{\mathrm{\max}}]
is a sigmoid function defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\theta}(z):={\nu_{\mathrm{\max}}}/{\{1+\exp(r(\nu_{0}-z))\}},\,z\in\mathbb{R}
.
Following the numerical experiment in
Buckwar et al. 2020
, we fix a part of the parameter vector
\displaystyle\theta
to

\displaystyle A=3.25,\,B=22,\,\nu_{0}=6,\,a=100,\,b=50,\,\nu_{\mathrm{max}}=5,\,r=0.56,\,\sigma_{1}=0.01,\,\sigma_{3}=1.

Then, we estimate the parameter
\displaystyle(C,\mu,\sigma_{2})
from observations of all coordinates of model (
5.1
) using the new contrast and the local Gaussian estimators. We set the true parameter values to

\displaystyle(C^{\dagger},\mu^{\dagger},\sigma_{2}^{\dagger})=(135.0,220.0,2000.0)
,
and generate synthetic datasets
\displaystyle Y_{\mathrm{JR}}
from the local Gaussian scheme (
2.6
) with discretisation step
\displaystyle 10^{-4}
on the time interval
\displaystyle[0,100]
. Then, we check the performance of the new contrast estimator versus the local Gaussian one in the following three scenarios for
\displaystyle n
and
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}
by subsampling from the synthetic datasets
\displaystyle Y_{\mathrm{JR}}
:

JR-1.

\displaystyle(n,\Delta_{n})=(1.25\times 10^{4},0.008)
with the time interval of observations
\displaystyle T=100
.

JR-2.

\displaystyle(n,\Delta_{n})=(2.5\times 10^{4},0.004)
with
\displaystyle T=100
.
JR-3.

\displaystyle(n,\Delta_{n})=(5\times 10^{4},0.002)
with
\displaystyle T=100
.

Since model (
5.1
) belongs in the class of stochastic damping Hamiltonian system (
4.7
) and matrix
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}
is diagonal, the correction term
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}
in the proposed contrast function is given as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\sum_{1\leq i\leq 3}2\Gamma_{\theta,ii}(\sigma_{i})^{2}\times\Bigl\{\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{(i,i)}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+\tfrac{1}{3}\mathcal{H}_{(i,i+3)}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{1}{6}\mathcal{H}_{(i+3,i)}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)+\tfrac{1}{8}\mathcal{H}_{(i+3,i+3)}(\Delta,x,y;\theta)\Bigr\},

with
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
.
As we noted in Section
4.1.4
,
partial derivatives of the non-linear function
\displaystyle G(X_{S,t},\theta)
are not required in the computation of the new contrast function. For the minimisation of the contrast, we use the Adam optimiser
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
with the following algorithmic specifications: (step-size) =
\displaystyle 0.01
, (exponential decay rate for the first moment estimates) =
\displaystyle 0.9
, (exponential decay rate for the second moment estimates) =
\displaystyle 0.999
, (additive term for numerical stability) =
\displaystyle 1\times 10^{-8}
and (number of iterations) =
\displaystyle 20,000
. In Table
1
, we summarise the mean and standard error from
\displaystyle 50
replicates of parameter estimates under scenario
JR-3
.
The table indicates that both the local Gaussian and the new contrast estimate accurately the drift parameters
\displaystyle(C,\mu)
, but the new contrast produces better results for the diffusion parameter
\displaystyle\sigma_{2}
as explained in Remark
4.3
. Thus, we focus on the estimation of the diffusion parameter
\displaystyle\sigma_{2}
and compare the proposed contrast estimator with, first, the local Gaussian estimator and, second, the estimator based on the quadratic variation of
\displaystyle X_{R,t}^{2}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\textstyle\hat{\sigma}_{2,n}^{\mathrm{QV}}:=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{T}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\bigl(X_{R,m\Delta_{n}}^{2}-X_{R,(m-1)\Delta_{n}}^{2}\bigr)^{2}}.

We summarise in Table
2
the mean and standard deviation of (true value of
\displaystyle\sigma_{2}
) - (estimator of
\displaystyle\sigma_{2}
) from
\displaystyle 50
replicates of estimates under scenario
JR-1,2,3
. Also, in Figure
1
we plot the root mean squared errors (RMSEs) from
\displaystyle 50
replicates of estimates of
\displaystyle\sigma_{2}
. Note that the new contrast estimator for
\displaystyle\sigma_{2}
gives faster convergence to the true value
\displaystyle\sigma_{2}^{\dagger}=2000
, in agreement with a relatively larger
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}
(as a function of
\displaystyle n
) permitted in the CLT for the new contrast – recall that for the CLT, the new contrast requires
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
versus
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
required by the local Gaussian contrast. We also observe that the new contrast estimator outperforms
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{2,n}^{\mathrm{QV}}
.
This is arguably not a coincidence for the particular experiment, as the contrast estimator makes use of observations from all co-ordinates (not only of
\displaystyle X_{R}^{2}
) via a likelihood-based contrast function,
and MLEs are well-understood to possess optimal asymptotic properties, e.g. due to the Cramér-Rao bound.

.

5.2
Low-frequency observation regime

We perform Bayesian inference, in a framework requiring data augmentation, for a susceptible-infected-recovered (SIR) model with a time-varying contact rate. The model is specified as the following 3-dimensional SDE:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left[\begin{array}[]{c}dS_{t}\\
dI_{t}\\
dC_{t}\end{array}\right]=\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}-\tfrac{C_{t}S_{t}I_{t}}{N}\\
\tfrac{C_{t}S_{t}I_{t}}{N}-\lambda I_{t}\\
\bigl(\alpha(\beta-\log C_{t})+\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\bigr)C_{t}\end{array}\!\!\right]dt+\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sqrt{\tfrac{C_{t}S_{t}I_{t}}{N}}&0&0\\
-\sqrt{\tfrac{C_{t}S_{t}I_{t}}{N}}&\sqrt{\lambda I_{t}}&0\\
0&0&\sigma\\
\end{array}\!\!\right]\,\left[\begin{array}[]{c}dB_{1,t}\\
dB_{2,t}\\
dB_{3,t}\\
\end{array}\right],\end{aligned}

(5.2)

where
\displaystyle S
,
\displaystyle I
account for the number of susceptible and infected individuals respectively,
\displaystyle C
for the contact rate,
\displaystyle N
is the population size and
\displaystyle\theta=(\alpha,\beta,\sigma,\lambda)
the parameter vector.
Motivated by an implementation in
Graham et al. 2022
, we consider the following setting:

•

Observations
\displaystyle Y=\{Y_{t}\}_{t=0,\ldots,T}
correspond to the number of daily infected individuals over the period of
\displaystyle 14
days, with time interval
\displaystyle\Delta=1
(day), and
they are assumed to be measured with additive Gaussian noise, as
\displaystyle Y_{t}=I_{t}+\sigma_{y}\,\varepsilon_{t}
,
\displaystyle 0\leq t\leq 13
, where
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{t}\sim\mathscr{N}(0,1)
and
\displaystyle\sigma_{y}>0
is constant.

•

For the parameter vector
\displaystyle\theta
and the initial condition
\displaystyle C_{0}
, priors are determined via
\displaystyle\theta=g_{\theta}(u)
and
\displaystyle C_{0}=g_{0}(u_{0})
, where
\displaystyle u
,
\displaystyle u_{0}
are vectors of independent standard normal random variables and
\displaystyle g_{\theta}
,
\displaystyle g_{0}
are some tractable functions.

•

Adopting a Bayesian data augmentation approach,
a numerical scheme is called upon to impute instances of model (
5.2
) at times separated by discretisation step
\displaystyle\delta
. The MCMC method is based on a non-centred imputation approach. That is, latent variables correspond to the Brownian increments driving the SDE,
and they are collected in a vector of (apriori) independent standard normal random variables
\displaystyle v
.

In the above setting, the posterior law of the
\displaystyle d_{q}
-dimensional vector
\displaystyle q=[u^{\top},u_{0}^{\top},v^{\top}]^{\top}
given the data
\displaystyle Y
has a tractable density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure (for more details, see Section 11 in the Supporting Material of
Graham et al. 2022
). Thus, one can apply standard Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) to sample from the posterior with energy function
\displaystyle H:\mathbb{R}^{d_{q}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{q}}\to\mathbb{R}
given as
\displaystyle H(q,p)=\ell(q)+\tfrac{1}{2}p^{\top}M^{-1}p
, where
\displaystyle\ell(q)
is the negative log-posterior density and
\displaystyle M
a diagonal mass matrix. We will run HMC on the posterior induced both by the Euler-Maruyama (EM) scheme and the proposed weak second order scheme, and we will show (numerically) that the involved bias is smaller in the case of the new scheme.

The details of the design of the experiment are as follows. We use the observations taken from
Anonymous 1978
as data
\displaystyle Y
. We fix
\displaystyle N=763
,
\displaystyle S_{0}=762
,
\displaystyle I_{0}=0
,
\displaystyle\sigma_{y}=5
. We assign priors
\displaystyle\log\alpha\sim\mathscr{N}(0,1)
,
\displaystyle\beta\sim\mathscr{N}(0,1)
,
\displaystyle\log\sigma\sim\mathscr{N}(-3,1)
,
\displaystyle\log\lambda\sim\mathscr{N}(0,1)
and
\displaystyle\log C_{0}\sim\mathscr{N}(0,1)
. We do not treat
\displaystyle\sigma_{y}
as unknown here due to the widely varying posterior scales induced by non-constant
\displaystyle\sigma_{y}
reported in
Graham et al. 2022
.
The (data-imputing) numerical schemes are applied to the log-transformation
\displaystyle X_{t}=[\log S_{t},\log I_{t},\log C_{t}]^{\top}
to ensure positiveness of
\displaystyle S
,
\displaystyle I
, and
\displaystyle C
, and avoid numerical issues with the square-root terms in the diffusion coefficient. We use a dynamic integration-time HMC implementation (
Betancourt 2017
) with a dual-averaging algorithm (
Hoffman et al. 2014
) to adapt the integrator step-size. For the
time-discretisation of the Hamiltonian dynamics we use the leapfrog integrator with Störmer-Verlet splitting. We set the mass matrix
\displaystyle M
to identity.
For each of the following three choices of numerical schemes, we run four HMC chains of 1,500 iterations with the first 500 iterations used as an adaptive warm-up phase:

SIR-baseline:

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{Numerical\,scheme},\delta_{M}\bigr)=\bigl(\mathrm{EM},0.001\bigr).

SIR-EM:

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{Numerical\,scheme},\delta_{M}\bigr)=\bigl(\mathrm{EM},0.05\bigr).

SIR-Weak2nd:

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{Numerical\,scheme},\delta_{M}\bigr)=\bigl(\mathrm{Weak\,second\,order\,scheme},0.05\bigr).

Results from the HMC implementations are summarised in Figure
2
and Table
3
. Figure
2
shows estimated pair-wise and marginal posteriors for parameters
\displaystyle\alpha
,
\displaystyle\beta
,
\displaystyle\sigma
,
\displaystyle\lambda
and
\displaystyle\log C_{0}
. Results for
SIR-EM
and
SIR-Weak2nd
are shown in separate plots, and each plot superimposes corresponding results from
SIR-baseline
, this latter scheme treated as providing the ‘correct’ posterior quantities due to the use of very small
\displaystyle\delta_{M}=0.001
.
Table
3
shows summary statistics that monitor the performance of the HMC algorithm, in particular, bulk effective sample size (ESS), tail ESS and improved
\displaystyle\hat{R}
with rank-normalisation and folding (see
Vehtari et al. 2021
for analytical definitions). These are computed from the non-warm-up steps of the four HMC chains. Note that for all parameters and choices of numerical schemes,
\displaystyle\hat{R}
is smaller than
\displaystyle 1.01
and ESS is larger than
\displaystyle 400
, as
Vehtari et al. 2021
recommend. One can thus be reasonably confident that the estimated posteriors shown in Figure
2
are reliable representations of the true ones.
In agreement with the analytical theory in this work, Figure
2
illustrates that for all parameters the estimated posteriors obtained via the weak second order scheme capture more accurately the ‘correct’ baseline posteriors than the corresponding ones obtained via the EM scheme.

(a)

Euler-Maruyama scheme

(b)

Weak second order scheme

The blue (left panel) and orange (right panel) histograms and contour plots are obtained by HMC that uses the EM scheme and the weak second order scheme, respectively, both with the same discretisation step
\displaystyle\delta_{M}=0.05
.
The black histograms and contour plots superimposed in both plots show the correct quantities, as obtained from HMC that uses the EM scheme with very small
\displaystyle\delta_{M}=10^{-3}
.

6
Conclusions

This work begins by putting forward weak second order sampling schemes for elliptic and hypo-elliptic SDEs.
Then, we develop a small time density expansion of the scheme as a proxy for the intractable SDE transition density. Via appropriate choice of the higher order expansion terms,
we have provided analytical results both: in a high-frequency classical setting, showcasing the advantageous rate of
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/3})
, achieved for hypo-elliptic models; and in a low-frequency Bayesian data augmentation setting, where we have deduced two local weak third order density schemes (
4.14
) and (
4.17
) and shown that the induced bias by the schemes is of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M^{-2})
when covering the fixed time interval
\displaystyle\Delta>0
with inner time-steps
\displaystyle\delta=\Delta/M,\,M>0
specified by user.

We iterate here that there is the flexibility to apply MCMC methods based only on the sampling schemes without reference to transition densities, via, e.g., non-centred model parameterisation approaches (see e.g.
Papaspiliopoulos et al. 2007
;
Beskos et al. 2015
), particle-filtering based MCMC methods
(
Andrieu et al. 2010
)
, and recent manifold-based algorithms for the case of observations without/with noise
(
Graham et al. 2022
)
. Via the derivation of approximate transition densities, one is still given the option to use the wealth of data augmentation methods for diffusion models that require such a density expression.

Appendix A
Notation

We set:

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{0}(\cdot,\beta,\gamma)=\big[\,V_{R,0}(\cdot,\beta)^{\top},V_{S,0}(\cdot,\gamma)^{\top}\,\big]^{\top},\quad V_{k}(\cdot,\sigma)=\big[\,V_{R,k}(\cdot,\sigma)^{\top},\mathbf{0}_{d_{S}}^{\top}\,\big]^{\top},\quad 1\leq k\leq d_{R}.

We set

\displaystyle V_{R}=V_{R}(x,\sigma)=\big[V_{R,1}(x,\sigma),\ldots,V_{R,d_{R}}(x,\sigma)\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}\times d_{R}}
, and define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle a_{R}(x,\sigma):=V_{R}(x,\sigma)V_{R}(x,\sigma)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{R}\times d_{R}}.

(A.1)

We consider the following differential operators, acting upon maps
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial_{u}:=\big[\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u^{1}},\ldots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u^{n}}\big]^{\top};\quad\partial^{2}_{u}=\partial_{u}\partial_{u}^{\top}\equiv\big(\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{i}\partial u^{j}}\big)_{i,j=1}^{n}.

If needed, we use superscripts to specify co-ordinates of a vector.
We denote by
\displaystyle\mathscr{S}
the space of functions

\displaystyle f:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
so that there exists a constant
\displaystyle C>0
such that
\displaystyle|f(h,x,\theta)|\leq Ch
for any
\displaystyle(h,x,\theta)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
,
and
\displaystyle\theta\mapsto f(h,x,\theta)
is continuous on
\displaystyle\Theta
for all
\displaystyle(h,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\nu_{i}(\Delta;\theta):=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{X_{R,t_{i}}-\mu_{R,\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)}{\sqrt{\Delta}}\\
\frac{X_{S,t_{i}}-\mu_{S,\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)}{\sqrt{\Delta^{3}}}\\
\end{bmatrix},\ \

for
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n
, where we have set:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta}(x;\theta)\equiv\begin{bmatrix}\mu_{R,\Delta}(x;\theta)\\
\mu_{S,\Delta}(x;\theta)\end{bmatrix}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}x_{R}+V_{R,0}(x,\beta)\Delta\\[2.84544pt]
x_{S}+V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\Delta+\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\end{bmatrix},

for
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta
and
\displaystyle x=[x_{R}^{\top},x_{S}^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We notice that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\nu_{i}^{j}(\Delta;\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]=\mathcal{O}(\Delta^{\frac{3}{2}}),\ \ 1\leq j\leq d,

(A.2)

and for
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1}\leq d_{\beta}
,
\displaystyle d_{\beta}+1\leq k_{2}\leq d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma}
,
\displaystyle d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma}+1\leq k_{3}\leq d_{\theta}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial_{\theta_{k_{1}}}\nu_{i}(\Delta;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}-\partial_{\theta_{k_{1}}}V_{R,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\beta)\sqrt{\Delta}\\[2.84544pt]
-\partial_{\theta_{k_{1}}}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\tfrac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\end{bmatrix};

(A.3)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial_{\theta_{k_{2}}}\nu_{i}(\Delta;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{d_{R}}\\[5.69046pt]
-\partial_{\theta_{k_{2}}}V_{S,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\gamma)\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta}}-\partial_{\theta_{k_{2}}}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\tfrac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\end{bmatrix};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}\nu_{i}(\Delta;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{d_{R}}\\[5.69046pt]
-\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\tfrac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\end{bmatrix}.

For
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\displaystyle(\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\gamma}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
, we set:

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\Sigma}_{\Delta}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\equiv{\Sigma}(\Delta,x;\sigma)\equiv\begin{bmatrix}{\Sigma}_{\Delta,RR}(x;\sigma)&{\Sigma}_{\Delta,RS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\\[2.84544pt]
{\Sigma}_{\Delta,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)&{\Sigma}_{\Delta,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\end{bmatrix},

where each block matrix is determined as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &{\Sigma}_{\Delta,RR}(x;\sigma)=\Delta\,a_{R}(x,\sigma),\ \ {\Sigma}_{\Delta,RS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)=\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\,a_{R}(x,\sigma)\bigl(\partial_{x_{R}}^{\top}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)^{\top};\\[5.69046pt]
&{\Sigma}_{\Delta,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)={\Sigma}_{\Delta,RS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)^{\top},\ \ {\Sigma}_{\Delta,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{3}\partial_{x_{R}}^{\top}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\,a_{R}(x,\sigma)\,\bigl(\partial_{x_{R}}^{\top}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)^{\top}.\vskip 5.69046pt\end{aligned}

We note that under conditions (
H3
)–(
H4
), matrices
\displaystyle\Sigma_{\Delta}(x;\gamma,\sigma)
,
\displaystyle\Sigma_{\Delta,RR}(x;\sigma)
and
\displaystyle\Sigma_{\Delta,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)
are positive definite for any
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle(\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\gamma}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
. Similarly, we use the following block expression for the inverse of matrix
\displaystyle{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma,\sigma)
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\equiv\begin{bmatrix}{\Lambda}_{1,RR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)&\Lambda_{1,RS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\\[2.84544pt]
\Lambda_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)&\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\end{bmatrix}.

In particular, we have:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)=4\cdot\Sigma_{1,SS}^{-1}(x;\gamma,\sigma).

(A.4)

We recall the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta)
and write

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta)=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{d}{G}_{i_{1}i_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta),

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta)
is the Hermite polynomial given as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{d}{\Sigma}_{1,i_{1}j_{1}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma){\Sigma}_{1,i_{2}j_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{2}}(\Delta;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-{\Sigma}_{1,i_{1}i_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma),

(A.5)

and the matrix
\displaystyle G(x;\theta)=(G_{i_{1}i_{2}}(x;\theta))_{1\leq i_{1},i_{2}\leq d}
,
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\theta\in\Theta
, is given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle G(x;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}G_{RR}(x;\theta)&G_{RS}(x;\theta)\\
G_{RS}(x;\theta)^{\top}&G_{SS}(x;\theta)\end{bmatrix},

(A.6)

with

\displaystyle\displaystyle G_{RR}(x;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\bigl(\hat{V}_{k}V_{R,0}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{0}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)\bigr)V_{R,k}(x,\theta)^{\top}+\tfrac{1}{4}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)^{\top};

\displaystyle\displaystyle G_{RS}(x;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{3}\hat{V}_{k}V_{R,0}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)\Bigr)\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)^{\top}+\tfrac{1}{12}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)\bigl(\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\bigr)^{\top}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{1}{12}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)^{\top};

\displaystyle\displaystyle G_{SS}(x;\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{8}\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\Bigr)^{\top}+\tfrac{1}{24}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)^{\top}.

We recall the notation for multiple stochastic integrals:

\displaystyle\displaystyle I_{\alpha}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t_{2}}dB_{\alpha_{1},t_{1}}\cdots dB_{\alpha_{l},t_{l}},\ \ t>0,

where
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{0,1,\ldots,d_{R}\}^{l},\;l\in\mathbb{N}
. Finally, we define
\displaystyle|\alpha|=l
for a multi-index
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{l}
,
\displaystyle l\in\mathbb{N}
.

Appendix B
Proofs of Theorem
4.1
&
4.2

B.1
Proof of Theorem
4.1

We prove consistency of the estimator
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}
in the complete observation regime (
1.2
). A number of required technical results are collected in Section
C.2
. To show consistency, a careful consideration of the structure of the covariance matrix of the model is needed, due to hypo-ellipticity.
Then, we proceed with our proof using a similar main strategy as in
Melnykova 2020
and
Gloter and Yoshida 2021
, i.e.:

Step 1.

We prove consistency for the estimator
\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{n}
. Using the consistency, we show that, if
\displaystyle n\to\infty,n\Delta\to\infty
and
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}-\gamma^{\dagger})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0.

(B.1)

Step 2.

Given the above convergence (
B.1
) and condition (
H6
), we prove consistency for
\displaystyle(\hat{\beta}_{n},\hat{\sigma}_{n})
.

B.1.1
Step 1.

Recall that

\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)
, and that the contrast function
\displaystyle\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)
is defined so that it is
minimized
at
\displaystyle\theta=\hat{\theta}_{n}
.
We have the following result whose proof is given in Section
C.2.1
:

Lemma B.1
.

Assume that conditions (
H2
)–(
H5
), (
H7
) and (
H8
) hold.
If
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)^{\top}{\Lambda}_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx),

uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta
.

Lemma
B.1
, together with conditions (
H1
) and (
H9
), gives directly that, as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
, then
\displaystyle\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}({\beta},\gamma^{\dagger},{\sigma})\rightarrow 0
, in probability, uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, thus

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}({\beta},\gamma,{\sigma})-\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}({\beta},\gamma^{\dagger},{\sigma})

(B.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)^{\top}\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx),

uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. We will now show that (
B.2
) implies
\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\gamma^{\dagger}
. From the identifiability condition (
H9
) and the definition of the contrast estimator, it holds that for any
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\left(|\hat{\gamma}_{n}-\gamma^{\dagger}|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\left(\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\theta}_{n})<\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\beta}_{n},\gamma^{\dagger},\hat{\sigma}_{n})\right).

(B.3)

Now we note that the right hand side of (
B.2
) must be no smaller than
\displaystyle 0
due to the non-negative definiteness of
\displaystyle{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\theta)
for every
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, and the convergence (
B.2
) holds uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. Hence, from condition (
H1
), i.e, the compactness of the parameter space
\displaystyle\Theta
, we have that if
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
and
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
, then

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\left(\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\theta}_{n})<\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\beta}_{n},\gamma^{\dagger},\hat{\sigma}_{n})\right)}\to 0,

which leads to the consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{n}
from inequality (
B.3
).

Next, we show that convergence (
B.1
) holds. The Taylor expansion of
\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\theta}_{n}),\;\hat{\theta}_{n}=(\hat{\beta}_{n},\hat{\gamma}_{n},\hat{\sigma}_{n})

around
\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\beta}_{n},\gamma^{\dagger},\hat{\sigma}_{n})
gives

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\gamma}\bigl(\hat{\beta}_{n},\hat{\sigma}_{n}\bigr)=B_{\gamma}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\times\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta}}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}-\gamma^{\dagger}),

(B.4)

where we have defined for
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\gamma}\bigl({\beta},\sigma\bigr):=-\tfrac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{n}\partial_{\gamma}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma),\quad B_{\gamma}(\theta):=\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\gamma}^{2}\ell_{n,\Delta}\bigl({\beta},\gamma^{\dagger}+\lambda(\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}),\sigma\bigr)d\lambda.

Then, we have the following result whose proof is given in Section
C.2.2
:

Lemma B.2
.

Assume that conditions (
H2
)–(
H5
), (
H7
) and (
H8
) hold. If
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\gamma}\bigl({\beta},\sigma\bigr)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0;

(B.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{\gamma}\bigl(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma\bigr)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\partial_{\gamma}V_{S,0}^{\top}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})\,\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\,\bigl(\partial_{\gamma}V_{S,0}^{\top}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})\bigr)^{\top}\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx).

(B.6)

uniformly in
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
.

Here, we notice that the right hand side of (
B.6
) is positive definite due to the positive definiteness of
\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)
for any
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\displaystyle(\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\gamma}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
. Hence, Lemma
B.2
and equation (
B.4
) give convergence (
B.1
).

B.1.2
Step 2.

For the proof, we define

\displaystyle T(\theta):=\tfrac{1}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta),\ \ Q(\theta):=\tfrac{1}{n\Delta}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)-\tfrac{1}{n\Delta}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\beta^{\dagger},\gamma,\sigma)
,
where
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. We will first consider the limit of
\displaystyle T(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)
to show consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n}
, and then that of
\displaystyle Q(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)
to prove consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{n}
. We note that these results will rely on the consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{n}
and convergence (
B.1
).
In particular, convergence (
B.1
) and condition (
H6
)
will allow for obtaining appropriate limits for
\displaystyle T(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)
and
\displaystyle Q(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)
.

Step 2-(i). Consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n}

We have the following result due to convergence (
B.1
).

Lemma B.3
.

Under conditions (
H2
)-(
H8
), it holds that as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle T(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\;\int\Bigl(\mathrm{tr}\bigl({\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)+\log|{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)|\Bigr)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx),

(B.7)

uniformly in
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
.

We show the proof of Lemma
B.3
in Section
C.2.3
.
Limit (
B.7
) with conditions (
H1
), (
H9
) will provide the consistency for
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n}
. In particular, we will show that
if
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
then,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\left(T(\hat{\theta}_{n})<T(\hat{\beta}_{n},\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma^{\dagger})\right)\to 0,

(B.8)

which implies consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n}
from the same discussion as in the proof of consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{n}
.
We denote by
\displaystyle U(\sigma)
the right-hand-side of (
B.7
), and will show that
\displaystyle\sigma\mapsto U(\sigma)
takes the minimum value at
\displaystyle\sigma=\sigma^{\dagger}
from the following discussion. We define

\displaystyle\displaystyle g(\sigma;x):=\mathrm{tr}\bigl({\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)+\log|{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)|.

Taking the derivative of
\displaystyle g(\sigma;x)
w.r.t.
\displaystyle\sigma_{i}
,
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq d_{\sigma}
, yields:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma_{i}}g(\sigma;x)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\partial_{\sigma_{i}}{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\Sigma_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)-\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\partial_{\sigma_{i}}{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\bigr),

where we used that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma_{i}}\log|{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)|=\mathrm{tr}\bigl({\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\partial_{\sigma_{i}}{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\bigr)=-\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\partial_{\sigma_{i}}{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\bigr),

obtained via consideration of the partial derivative of
\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\bigl({\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\bigr)\equiv\mathrm{tr}(I_{d})
. Hence, we have
\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}g(\sigma;x)|_{\sigma=\sigma^{\dagger}}=0

for all
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and then,

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}U(\sigma)|_{\sigma=\sigma^{\dagger}}=0.

From the compactness of the parameter space (
H1
), we obtain the limit (
B.8
), and deduce the consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n}
.

Step 2-(ii). Consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{n}

Due to convergence (
B.1
), and the consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{n}
and
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n}
, we have the following result with proof provided in Section
C.2.4
.

Lemma B.4
.

Under conditions (
H2
)–(
H8
), it holds that as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle Q(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\hat{\sigma}_{n})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\bigl(e(x;\theta^{\dagger})-e(x;\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)^{\top}{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigl(e(x;\theta^{\dagger})-e(x;\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx),

uniformly in
\displaystyle\beta\in\Theta_{\beta}
, where
\displaystyle e(\cdot;\theta):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\theta\in\Theta
, is defined as

\displaystyle\displaystyle e(x;\theta)=\big[\,V_{R,0}(x,\beta)^{\top},\,\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)^{\top}\,\big]^{\top},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

From this convergence, together with conditions (
H1
) and (
H9
), we deduce
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\beta^{\dagger}
using the same argument as for the consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{n}
.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
4.2

We now prove asymptotic normality for estimator
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}
. Throughout the proof, we consider the hypo-elliptic SDE scenario,
\displaystyle d_{R}<d
.

The Taylor expansion of
\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\theta}_{n})
,
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}=(\hat{\beta}_{n},\hat{\gamma}_{n},\hat{\sigma}_{n})
, around the true parameter vector
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}=(\beta^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})
yields

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{n,\Delta}(\theta^{\dagger})=\int_{0}^{1}B_{n,\Delta}\bigl(\theta^{\dagger}+\lambda(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\dagger})\bigr)\,d\lambda\times C_{n,\Delta}(\hat{\theta}_{n}),

(B.9)

where, for
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, we have defined

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{n,\Delta}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=-\Big[\,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n\Delta}}\,\partial_{\beta}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)^{\top},\,\sqrt{\tfrac{\Delta}{n}}\,\partial_{\gamma}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)^{\top},\,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,\partial_{\sigma}\,\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)^{\top}\,\Big]^{\top};

\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{n,\Delta}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=M_{n,\Delta}\partial^{2}_{\theta}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)M_{n,\Delta},\quad C_{n,\Delta}(\theta):=\Big[\,\sqrt{n\Delta}\,({\beta}-\beta^{\dagger})^{\top},\,\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\Delta}}\,(\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger})^{\top},\,\sqrt{n}\,(\sigma-\sigma^{\dagger})^{\top}\,\Big]^{\top},

with a scaling matrix

\displaystyle M_{n,\Delta}:=\mathrm{diag}(v_{n,\Delta})
,
where
\displaystyle v_{n,\Delta}
is a
\displaystyle d_{\theta}
-dimensional vector given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle v_{n,\Delta}=\Bigl[\;\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n\Delta}},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n\Delta}}}_{d_{\beta}-\mathrm{dim}},\;\underbrace{\sqrt{\tfrac{\Delta}{n}},\ldots,\sqrt{\tfrac{\Delta}{n}}}_{d_{\gamma}-\mathrm{dim}},\;\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}_{d_{\sigma}-\mathrm{dim}}\;\Bigr]^{\top},\quad d_{\theta}=d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma}+d_{\sigma}.

We will continue from (
B.9
), using the the following results.

Lemma B.5
.

Under conditions (
H2
)–(
H5
), (
H7
) and (
H8
), it holds that as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
and
\displaystyle n\Delta^{3}\to 0
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\theta^{\dagger})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\mathscr{N}\bigl(0,4I(\theta^{\dagger})\bigr).

Lemma B.6
.

Under conditions (
H1
)–(
H9
), it holds that as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{n,\Delta}\bigl(\theta^{\dagger}+\lambda(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\dagger})\bigr)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}2I(\theta^{\dagger}),

uniformly in
\displaystyle\lambda\in(0,1]
.

We provide the proofs in Section
C.3
.
Via these results, we immediately obtain the stated asymptotic normality of
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}
.

Appendix C
Proof of Technical Results for Theorem
4.1
and
4.2

C.1
Auxiliary Results

Before we prove Lemmas
B.1
–
B.6
, we prepare some auxiliary results in this section.

Lemma C.1
.

Assume Conditions (
H3
)-(
H4
) holds. For any
\displaystyle x=(x_{R},x_{S})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\displaystyle(\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\gamma}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\partial^{\top}_{x_{R}}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)=\mathbf{0}_{d_{S}\times d_{R}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\partial^{\top}_{x_{R}}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\Sigma_{1,RR}(x;\sigma)\Sigma_{1,RR}^{-1}(x;\sigma)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\partial^{\top}_{x_{R}}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\Sigma_{1,RR}(x;\sigma)\Sigma_{1,RR}^{-1}(x;\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=\Bigl(\Lambda_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\Sigma_{1,RR}(x;\sigma)+\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\Sigma_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\Bigr)\Sigma_{1,RR}^{-1}(x;\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=\mathbf{0}_{d_{S}\times d_{R}},

where we have used the equations:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Sigma_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}\partial^{\top}_{x_{R}}V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\Sigma_{1,RR}(x;\sigma);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\Sigma_{1,RR}(x;\sigma)+\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\Sigma_{1,SR}(x;\gamma,\sigma)=\mathbf{0}_{d_{S}\times d_{R}}.

∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\displaystyle Y_{t_{i}}
,
\displaystyle U
be random variables, with
\displaystyle Y_{t_{i}}
being
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}}
-measurable. The following two convergences imply
\displaystyle\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{t_{i}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}U}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[Y_{t_{i}}|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}U,\ \ \ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\big[\,\bigl(Y_{t_{i}}\bigr)^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\,\big]\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0.

Proof.

See Lemma 9 in
Genon-Catalot and Jacod 1993
.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable with derivatives of polynomial growth in
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. Assume that conditions
(
H2
), (
H5
), (
H7
), (
H8
) hold. Then, as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int f(x,\theta)\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx),

uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
.

Proof.

This is a multivariate version of Lemma 8 in
Kessler 1997
, so we omit the proof.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n
,
\displaystyle 1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d
and
\displaystyle\Delta>0,\,\theta\in\Theta
. Under condition (
H2
),
it holds that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Bigl[\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{2}}(\Delta;\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)+\Xi_{j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\Delta+\mathcal{E}(\Delta^{2},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{S}

and a
\displaystyle d\times d
matrix
\displaystyle\Xi=\Xi(x,\theta)
, such that for
\displaystyle 1\leq l_{1},l_{2}\leq d_{R}
,
\displaystyle d_{R}+1\leq l_{3},l_{4}\leq d
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\Xi}_{l_{1}l_{2}}(x;\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\big\{\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)V_{k}^{l_{2}}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{l_{2}}(x,\theta)V_{k}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\bigl\{\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)V_{k}^{l_{2}}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{l_{2}}(x,\theta)V_{k}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)\bigl\}\;+\;\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{l_{2}}(x,\theta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\Xi}_{l_{1}l_{3}}(x;\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{3}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{0}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}V_{k_{1}}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{6}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}V_{k}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)\bigl(\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta)+\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta)\bigr)+\tfrac{1}{6}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{l_{1}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\Xi}_{l_{3}l_{1}}(x;\theta)={\Xi}_{l_{1}l_{3}}(x;\theta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\Xi}_{l_{3}l_{4}}(x;\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta)\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{l_{4}}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{8}\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{l_{4}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta)+\tfrac{1}{8}\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta)\Bigr)\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{l_{4}}(x,\theta)\;+\;\tfrac{1}{12}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{l_{3}}(x,\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{l_{4}}(x,\theta).

Proof.

The stochastic Itô-Taylor expansion of

\displaystyle X_{\Delta}=\bigl[X_{R,\Delta}^{\top},X_{S,\Delta}^{\top}\bigr]^{\top}
gives as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle X_{R,\Delta}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=x_{R}+\sum_{k=0}^{d_{R}}V_{R,k}(x,\theta)B_{k,\Delta}+\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=0}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{R,k_{2}}(x,\theta)I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta)+\rho_{R}(\Delta,\theta);

(C.1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle X_{S,\Delta}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=x_{S}+V_{S,0}(x,\theta)\Delta+\sum_{k=0}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)I_{(k,0)}(\Delta)+\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=0}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta)+\rho_{S}(\Delta,\theta),

where

\displaystyle\rho_{R}(\Delta,\theta):=\rho_{R,1}(\Delta,\theta)+\rho_{R,2}(\Delta,\theta)

and

\displaystyle\rho_{S}(t,\theta):=\rho_{S,1}(\Delta,\theta)+\rho_{S,2}(\Delta,\theta)

with

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho_{R,1}(\Delta,\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{k=3,4,5}\,\,\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1,\ldots,d_{R}\}^{k}}\hat{V}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\hat{V}_{\alpha_{k-1}}V_{R,\alpha_{k}}(x,\theta)I_{\alpha}(\Delta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho_{S,1}(\Delta,\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{k=3,4}\,\,\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1,\ldots,d_{R}\}^{k}}\hat{V}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\hat{V}_{\alpha_{k}}V_{S,0}(x,\theta)I_{(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k},0)}(\Delta).

Under condition (
H2
), there exist constants
\displaystyle c_{1},c_{2}>0
so that

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\rho_{R,2}(\Delta,\theta)]\bigr|\leq c_{1}\Delta^{3}

and

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\rho_{S,2}(\Delta,\theta)]\bigr|\leq c_{2}\Delta^{3}
.
From expansion (
C.1
) we have for
\displaystyle 1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d_{R}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Bigl[\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{2}}(\Delta;\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)+\tfrac{\Delta}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}V_{k}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\bigl(\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{\Delta}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\bigl(\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\bigr)V_{k}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{\Delta}{2}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Delta^{2},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\in\mathscr{S}
, where we have used that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}B_{k_{2},\Delta}]=\Delta\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}},\ \ \mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}I_{(k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}I_{(0,k_{2})}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta)I_{(k_{3},k_{4})}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{3},k_{2}=k_{4}},\ \ \mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{2},k_{3})}(\Delta)]=0,

for
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}\leq d_{R}
, and that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\bigl(X_{R,t_{i}}^{j_{1}}-\mu_{R,\Delta}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr)\rho_{R}^{j_{2}}(\Delta,\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]=\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Delta^{3},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),\ \ 1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d_{R},

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
.
Similarly, for
\displaystyle 1\leq j_{1}\leq d_{R},\;d_{R}+1\leq j_{2}\leq d
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Bigl[\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{2}}(\Delta;\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)+\Delta\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{3}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}V_{k}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\Bigr)\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{\Delta}{6}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}V_{k}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\bigl(\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{\Delta}{6}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\mathcal{E}_{3}(\Delta^{2},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}\in\mathscr{S}
, where we used

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}I_{(k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}I_{(k_{2},0,0)}(\Delta)]=\mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}I_{(0,k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{6}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}},\ \ \mathbb{E}[B_{k_{1},\Delta}I_{(k_{2},k_{3},0)}(\Delta)]=0;

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{3}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}},\ \ \mathbb{E}[I_{(0,k_{1})}(\Delta)I_{(k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{6}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\Delta)I_{(k_{3},k_{4},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{6}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{3},k_{2}=k_{4}},

for
\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}\leq d_{R}
, and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\bigl(X_{R,t_{i}}^{j_{1}}-\mu_{R,\Delta}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr)\rho_{S}^{j_{2}}(\Delta,\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]=\mathcal{E}_{4}(\Delta^{4},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\rho_{R}^{j_{1}}(\Delta,\theta)\bigl(X_{S,t_{i}}^{j_{2}}-\mu_{S,\Delta}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]=\mathcal{E}_{5}(\Delta^{4},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

for
\displaystyle 1\leq j_{1}\leq d_{R},\;1\leq j_{2}\leq d_{S}
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{4},\mathcal{E}_{5}\in\mathscr{S}
. Finally, for
\displaystyle d_{R}+1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Bigl[\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{2}}(\Delta;\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle={\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)+\Delta\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{8}\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\Delta\sum_{k=1}^{d_{R}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{8}\hat{V}_{k}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\tfrac{1}{6}\hat{V}_{0}\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\Bigr)\hat{V}_{k}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{\Delta}{12}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{d_{R}}\hat{V}_{k_{1}}V_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\hat{V}_{k_{1}}\hat{V}_{k_{2}}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\mathcal{E}_{6}(\Delta^{2},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{6}\in\mathscr{S}
, where we used

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{3}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}},\ \ \mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{2},0,0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{4}}{8}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},0)}(\Delta)I_{(0,k_{2},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{4}}{6}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}},\ \ \mathbb{E}[I_{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}(\Delta)I_{(k_{3},k_{4},0)}(\Delta)]=\tfrac{\Delta^{4}}{12}\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}=k_{3},k_{2}=k_{4}},

and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\bigl(X_{S,t_{i}}^{j_{1}}-\mu_{S,\Delta}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr)\rho_{S}^{j_{2}}(\Delta,\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]=\mathcal{E}_{7}(\Delta^{5},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),\ \ 1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d_{S},

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{7}\in\mathscr{S}
. The proof is now complete.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

Let
\displaystyle 1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d
and let
\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy the regularity assumption of Lemma
C.3
. Under conditions (
H2
), (
H5
), (
H7
) and (
H8
), it holds that as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta,\theta^{\dagger})\nu_{i}^{j_{2}}(\Delta,\theta^{\dagger})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int f(x,\theta){\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

(C.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{n\sqrt{\Delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta,\theta^{\dagger})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,

(C.3)

uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
.

Proof.

First, we will prove (
C.2
).
Let

\xi_{i}^{j_{1}j_{2}}(\theta):=\tfrac{1}{n}f({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta,\theta^{\dagger})\nu_{i}^{j_{2}}(\Delta,\theta^{\dagger}),\ \ 1\leq i\leq n,\;1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d.

We have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\bigl[\xi_{i}^{j_{1}j_{2}}(\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta){\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})+\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\bigl[(\xi_{i}^{j_{1}j_{2}}(\theta))^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{2}(1,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta).

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
.
Hence, it follows from Lemma
C.3
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\bigl[\xi_{i}^{j_{1}j_{2}}(\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int f(x,\theta){\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\bigl[(\xi_{i}^{j_{1}j_{2}}(\theta))^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0.

Then, Lemma
C.2
yields convergence (
C.2
). Next, we turn to (
C.3
). Let

\eta_{i}^{j_{1}}(\theta):=\tfrac{1}{n\sqrt{\Delta}}f({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta,\theta^{\dagger}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq n,\quad 1\leq j_{1}\leq d.

Equation (
A.2
) and Lemma
C.3
give that, as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\bigl[\eta_{i}^{j_{1}}(\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{3}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0;

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}\bigl[(\eta_{i}^{j_{1}}(\theta))^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\;\tfrac{1}{n\Delta}\times\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{4}(1,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3},\,\mathcal{E}_{4}\in\mathscr{S}
.
Finally, we obtain convergence (
C.3
) due to Lemma
C.2
.
∎

Lemma C.6
.

Let

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n
,

\displaystyle 1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq d
,

\displaystyle 1\leq k_{1}\leq d_{\beta}
,

\displaystyle d_{\beta}+1\leq k_{2}\leq d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma}
,

\displaystyle d_{\beta}+d_{\gamma}+1\leq k_{3}\leq d_{\theta}
.
Under condition (
H2
), it holds that for any
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\mathcal{H}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},X_{t_{i}};\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},\theta);

(C.4)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\partial_{\theta_{k_{1}}}\mathcal{H}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},X_{t_{i}};\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Delta^{2},X_{t_{i-1}},\theta);

(C.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\partial_{\theta_{k_{2}}}\mathcal{H}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},X_{t_{i}};\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta_{k_{2}}}{\Sigma}^{-1}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}(X_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)+\mathcal{E}_{3}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},\theta);

(C.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}\mathcal{H}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},X_{t_{i}};\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}{\Sigma}^{-1}_{1,j_{1},j_{2}}(X_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)+\mathcal{E}_{4}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

(C.7)

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{3},\mathcal{E}_{4}\in\mathscr{S}
.

Proof.

We first check (
C.4
). Using Lemma
C.4
with (
A
), we immediately have, for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{5}\in\mathscr{S}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\mathcal{H}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},X_{t_{i}};\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{d}{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}i_{1}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma){\Sigma}_{1,j_{2}i_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma){\Sigma}_{1,i_{1}i_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)+\mathcal{E}_{5}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)-{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta).

Next, we turn to (
C.5
). We have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\partial_{\theta_{k_{1}}}\mathcal{H}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{d}{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}i_{1}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma){\Sigma}_{1,j_{2}i_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\partial_{\theta_{k_{1}}}\nu_{i}^{i_{1}}(\Delta;\theta)\nu_{i}^{i_{2}}(\Delta;\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{d}{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}i_{1}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma){\Sigma}_{1,j_{2}i_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\nu_{i}^{i_{1}}(\Delta;\theta)\partial_{\theta_{k_{1}}}\nu_{i}^{i_{2}}(\Delta;\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Delta^{2},{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

where we used (
A.2
) and (
A.3
). Finally, let show (
C.7
). The formula (
C.6
) is derived in the similar way to (
C.7
) and we will omit the derivation. From Lemma
C.4
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\bigl[\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}\mathcal{H}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i-1}}\bigr]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{d}\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}\left({\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}i_{1}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma){\Sigma}_{1,j_{2}i_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\right){\Sigma}_{1,i_{1}i_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)-\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)+\mathcal{E}_{4}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta_{k_{3}}}{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)+\mathcal{E}_{4}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta).

The proof is now complete.
∎

C.2
Proof of Technical Results for Theorem 4.3

C.2.1
Proof of Lemma
B.1

We define
\displaystyle\textstyle{\frac{\Delta}{n}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\theta)}=\Gamma_{1}(\theta)+\Gamma_{2}(\theta)+\Gamma_{3}(\theta)
, where

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(X_{t_{i}}-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(X_{t_{i}}-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log|{\Sigma}_{1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)|,\ \ \ \Gamma_{3}(\theta)=-\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{2}(\Delta,X_{t_{i-1}},X_{t_{i}};\theta).

We will show that, as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)^{\top}\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}(\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,\ \ \ \Gamma_{3}(\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,

uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta
.

From Lemma
C.3
, we immediately have

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}(\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,

uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. We next turn to
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\theta)
. We introduce

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\theta)=\Gamma_{1,1}(\theta)+\Gamma_{1,2}(\theta)+\Gamma_{1,3}(\theta)

with

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{1,1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nu_{i}(\Delta;\theta^{\dagger})^{\top}{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\nu_{i}(\Delta;\theta^{\dagger});

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{1,2}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{2\Delta}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(X_{t_{i}}-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta^{\dagger})\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta^{\dagger})-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{1,3}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{\Delta}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta^{\dagger})-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta^{\dagger})-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr).

From Lemmas
C.2
,
C.3
and
C.5
, we immediately have
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1,k}(\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,\;k=1,2.
Since we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{1,3}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)^{\top}\Lambda_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)\Bigl|_{x={X}_{t_{i-1}}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta),

for
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{S}
, Lemmas
C.2
and
C.3
yield

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{1,3}(\theta)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)^{\top}{\Lambda}_{1,SS}(x;\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(V_{S,0}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{S,0}(x,\gamma)\bigr)\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx),

uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. Thus, we have the convergence of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\theta)
in probability. Similarly, for
\displaystyle\Gamma_{3}(\theta)
, due to term
\displaystyle\Delta
in front of the definition of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{3}(\theta)
, we have
\displaystyle\Gamma_{3}(\theta)\rightarrow 0
, in probability under
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}
,
uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. The proof is now complete.

C.2.2
Proof of Lemma
B.2

Convergence (
B.5
) is deduced from the proof of Lemma
B.6
in Section
C.3.2
, so here we only provide the proof of convergence (
B.6
).
We have for any
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\gamma}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tfrac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{n}\partial_{\gamma_{k}}\ell_{n,\Delta}(\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)=A_{\gamma_{k}}^{(1)}(\beta,\sigma)+A_{\gamma_{k}}^{(2)}(\beta,\sigma),\ \ 1\leq k\leq d_{\gamma},

where we have set:

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\gamma_{k}}^{(1)}(\beta,\sigma)\equiv\tfrac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\biggl\{-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}\leq d\\
d_{R}+1\leq j_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\Sigma^{-1}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\tfrac{\partial_{\gamma_{k}}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\gamma^{\dagger})}{\sqrt{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad-\sqrt{\Delta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}\leq d\\
d_{R}+1\leq j_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\Sigma^{-1}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\partial_{\gamma_{k}}\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad+\nu_{i}(\Delta,\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)^{\top}\partial_{\gamma_{k}}\Sigma^{-1}_{1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\nu_{i}(\Delta,\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)+\partial_{\gamma_{k}}\log|\Sigma_{1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)|\biggr\};

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\gamma_{k}}^{(2)}(\beta,\sigma)\equiv-\tfrac{2\sqrt{\Delta^{3}}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{\gamma_{k}}\Phi_{2}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma).

Since we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\nu_{i}(\Delta,\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)=\nu_{i}(\Delta,\theta^{\dagger})+\sqrt{\Delta}\begin{bmatrix}V_{R,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\beta^{\dagger})-V_{R,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\beta)\\[2.84544pt]
\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\theta^{\dagger})-\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{V}_{0}V_{S,0}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\beta,\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)\end{bmatrix},

we immediately obtain from Lemmas
C.3
,
C.5
that, as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
and
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
, then
\displaystyle A_{\gamma_{k}}^{(1)}(\beta,\sigma)\rightarrow 0
, in probability under
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}
, uniformly in
\displaystyle\beta
and
\displaystyle\sigma
, for all
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq d_{\gamma}
. Similarly, due to the presence of
\displaystyle\sqrt{\Delta^{3}}
,
we obtain
\displaystyle A_{\gamma_{k}}^{(2)}(\beta,\sigma)\rightarrow 0
, in probability under
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}
, uniformly in
\displaystyle\beta
and
\displaystyle\sigma
. We now obtain convergence (
B.5
) and the proof is complete.

C.2.3
Proof of Lemma
B.1

We write
\displaystyle\textstyle{T(\theta)=\sum_{l=1}^{3}T_{l}(\theta)},\;\theta\in\Theta
, where

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(X_{t_{i}}-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)\bigl(X_{t_{i}}-\mu_{\Delta}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\theta)\bigr);

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log|{\Sigma}_{1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\gamma,\sigma)|,\ \ \ T_{3}(\theta):=-\tfrac{2\Delta}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{2}(\Delta,{X}_{t_{i-1}},{X}_{t_{i}};\theta).

We will show that as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\mathrm{tr}\bigl({\Sigma}^{-1}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

(C.8)

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{2}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\int\log|{\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma)|\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

(C.9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{3}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0,

(C.10)

uniformly in
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
.

Proof of (
C.8
) and (
C.9
).

Via Lemma
C.3
, we immediately obtain convergence (
C.9
). We will check convergence (
C.8
). We have

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1}(\theta)=T_{1,1}(\theta)+T_{1,2}(\theta)+T_{1,3}(\theta),

(C.11)

where
\displaystyle\textstyle{T_{1,i}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\Delta}\Gamma_{1,i}(\theta),\;i=1,2,3}
.
From Lemma
C.5
and the continuous mapping theorem, as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1,1}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{d}\int{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}^{-1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger}\,)\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx)=\int\mathrm{tr}\bigl({\Sigma}^{-1}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma){\Sigma}_{1}(x;\gamma^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\bigr)\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx),

uniformly in
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
. We look at the convergence of term
\displaystyle T_{1,2}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)
. We have

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1,2}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\biggl\{{2\sqrt{\Delta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}\leq d\\
1\leq j_{2}\leq d_{R}\end{subarray}}\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta^{\dagger}){\Sigma}^{-1}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}(x;\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\bigl(V_{0}^{j_{2}}(x,\beta^{\dagger})-V_{0}^{j_{2}}(x,\beta)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}\leq d\\
d_{R}+1\leq j_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta^{\dagger}){\Sigma}^{-1}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}(x;\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\frac{V_{0}^{j_{2}}(x,\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{0}^{j_{2}}(x,\hat{\gamma}_{n})}{\sqrt{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+{\sqrt{\Delta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}\leq d\\
d_{R}+1\leq j_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}\nu_{i}^{j_{1}}(\Delta;\theta^{\dagger}){\Sigma}^{-1}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}(x;\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\bigl(\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{j_{2}}(x,\theta^{\dagger})-\hat{V}_{0}V_{0}^{j_{2}}\left(x,(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\right)\bigr)\biggr\}\bigr|_{x={X}_{t_{i-1}}}.

Using Lemma
C.5
, we have
\displaystyle T_{1,2}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}0
, uniformly in
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{\beta}\times\Theta_{\sigma}
. Next, we turn to term
\displaystyle T_{1,3}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)
. We have

\displaystyle\textstyle{T_{1,3}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)=\sum_{l=1}^{3}T_{1,3,l}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)}
, where

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1,3,1}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}\leq d_{R}\\
d_{R}+1\leq j_{2}\leq d\end{subarray}}{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\bigl(V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\beta^{\dagger})-V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\beta)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\bigl(V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\gamma}_{n})\bigr);

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1,3,2}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=d_{R}+1}^{d}{\Sigma}_{1,j_{1}j_{2}}^{-1}({X}_{t_{i-1}};\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)\frac{\bigl(V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{0}^{j_{1}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\gamma}_{n})\bigr)}{\sqrt{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{\bigl(V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\gamma^{\dagger})-V_{0}^{j_{2}}({X}_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\gamma}_{n})\bigr)}{\sqrt{\Delta}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle T_{1,3,3}(\beta,\hat{\gamma}_{n},\sigma)
